- India – location - Introduction to India's location - - - Overview - The mainland of India, extends from Kashmir in the north to Kanniyakumari in the south and Arunachal Pradesh in the east to Gujarat in the west - India’s territorial limit further extends towards the sea upto 12 nautical miles (about 21.9 km) from the coast (See the box for conversion) - Conversion table -
Statute [law / regulation] Mile Nautical [naval] Mile
A unit of distance used on land, based on the imperial system of measurement. A unit of distance used in marine and air navigation, defined by the Earth's geometry.
Here minute doesn't mean time, but it is a subunit of degree, 1 degree = 60 minute, 1 minute = 60 seconds.
1 Statute Mile = about 1.6 km (1.609 km)
  • 1 nautical mile = length of one minute of latitude along any meridian of the Earth.
  • The Earth is divided into 360 degrees of latitude
  • Each degree is divided into 60 minutes
  • Total minutes from Equator to Pole
    • 90 degrees × 60 minutes = 5,400 minutes
  • Distance from Equator to Pole ≈ 10,000 km
  • Therefore
    • 10,000 km ÷ 5,400 ≈ 1.852 km
  • Hence
    • 1 nautical mile = 1.852 kilometres
Used in land transport, surveying, and road maps. Used in global positioning, navigation charts, and aviation routes.
- Latitudinal and longitudinal extent - If you work out the latitudinal and longitudinal extent of India, they are roughly about 30 degrees, whereas the actual distance measured from north to south extremity is 3,214 km, and that from east to west is only 2,933 km - This difference is based on the fact that the distance between two longitudes decreases towards the poles whereas the distance between two latitudes remains the same everywhere - Latitude extent - Our southern boundary extends upto 6°45' N latitude in the Bay of Bengal - From the values of latitude, it is understood that the southern part of the country lies within the tropics and the northern part lies in the sub-tropical zone or the warm temperate zone - This location is responsible for large variations in land forms, climate, soil types and natural vegetation in the country - Longitudinal extent - From the values of longitude, it is quite discernible that there is a variation of nearly 30 degrees, which causes a time difference of nearly two hours between the easternmost and the westernmost parts of our country - Timezone - You are familiar with the concept of Indian Standard Time (IST) - While the sun rises in the northeastern states about two hours earlier as compared to Jaisalmer, the watches in Dibrugarh, Imphal in the east and Jaisalmer, Bhopal or Chennai in the other parts of India show the same time - Why does this happen? - There is a general understanding among the countries of the world to select the standard meridian in multiples of 7°30' of longitude - That is why 82°30' E has been selected as the ‘standard meridian’ of India - Indian Standard Time is ahead of Greenwich Mean Time by 5 hours and 30 minutes - There are some countries where there are more than one standard meridian due to their vast east-to-west extent - For example, the USA has seven time zones - Name a few places in India through which the standard meridian passes? - The Indian Standard Meridian (82.5° E) passes through Mirzapur in Mirzapur district of Uttar Pradesh, and then through the states of Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha, and Andhra Pradesh. - - - India's size and ranking - India with its area of 3.28 million sq. km accounts for 2.4 per cent of the world’s land surface area and stands as the seventh largest country in the world - Top 10 countries with size -

- India has less land yet similar population to China - Although China is geographically larger, only about 12–15% of its land is habitable - With nearly 30% occupied by deserts (Gobi and Taklamakan) - Around 25–30% by high mountains and the Tibetan Plateau, and additional areas under cold or arid conditions - Whereas India has nearly 55–60% habitable land, with only about 10–12% desert (Thar), ~15–18% mountains and highlands (Himalayas and allied ranges), and limited permanently ice-covered areas, making India far more suitable for dense human settlement. - Size - Physical diversity - The size of India has endowed her with great physical diversity like - The presence of lofty mountains in the north - Large rivers such as Ganga, Brahmaputra, Mahanadi, Krishna, Godavari and Kaveri - Green forested hills in northeast and south India - The vast sandy expanse of Marusthali - Bounded by the Himalayas in the north - Hindukush and Sulaiman ranges in the northwest - Purvachal hills in the north-east - By the large expanse of the Indian ocean in the south - It forms a great geographic entity known as the Indian subcontinent - - It includes the countries — Pakistan, Nepal, Bhutan, Bangladesh and India - Barriers to entry in subcontinent - The Himalayas, together with other ranges, have acted as a formidable physical barrier in the past - Except for a few mountain passes such as the Khyber, the Bolan, the Shipkila, the Nathula, the Bomdila, etc. it was difficult to cross it - It has contributed towards the evolving of a unique regional identity of the Indian subcontinent - By referring to the physical map of India you can now describe the physical variations which you would come across while travelling from Kashmir to Kanniyakumari and from Jaisalmer in Rajasthan to Imphal in Manipur - Coastline - - Peninsular part of India extends towards the Indian Ocean - This has provided the country with a coastline of 6,100 km in the mainland and 7,517 km in the entire geographical coast of the mainland plus the island groups Andaman and Nicobar located in the Bay of Bengal and the Lakshadweep in the Arabian Sea - India’s total coastline has been recalculated as 11,098.81 kilometres, up from the long-used figure of 7,516.6 km. - New measurements use higher resolution data and modern GIS tools, capturing intricate shoreline details and offshore island coastlines, hence the increase of nearly 48 per cent. - Thus India, as a country, is a physically diverse land providing occurrence of varied resources - Bhuvan-NRSC - School Bhuvan NCERT is a portal providing map-based learning to bring awareness among the students about country’s natural resources, environment and their role in sustainable development - It is an initiative of Bhuvan-NRSC/ISRO, based on NCERT syllabus - India and its neighbours - Location and maritime links - India is located in the south-central part of the continent of Asia, bordering the Indian ocean and its two arms extending in the form of Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea - This maritime location of Peninsular India has provided links to its neighbouring regions through the sea and air routes - Neighbours -

- Structure and physiography - Introduction to earth's geological history - The earth and its landforms that we see today have evolved over a very long time - Current scientific estimation shows that the Earth is approximately 4.54 billion years old - Over these long years, it has undergone many changes brought about primarily by the endogenic and exogenic forces - These forces have played a significant role in giving shape to various surface and subsurface features of the earth - You have already studied about the Plate Tectonics and the movement of the Earth’s plates in the book Fundamentals of Physical Geography (NCERT, 2006) - The Indian plate was to the south of the equator millions of years ago - It was much larger in size and the Australian plate was a part of it - Over millions of years, this plate broke into many parts and the Australian plate moved towards the southeastern direction and the Indian plate to the north - This northward movement of the Indian plate is still continuing and it has significant consequences on the physical environment of the Indian subcontinent - Geological divisions of India - It is primarily through the interplay of these endogenic and exogenic forces and lateral movements of the plates that the present geological structure and geomorphologic processes active in the Indian subcontinent came into existence - Based on the variations in its geological structure and formations, India can be divided into three geological divisions. These geological regions broadly follow the physical features: 1. The Peninsular Block - - Geographic Extent - The northern boundary of the Peninsular Block may be taken as an irregular line running from Kachchh along the western flank of the Aravali Range near Delhi and then roughly parallel to the Yamuna and the Ganga as far as the Rajmahal Hills and the Ganga delta - Apart from these, the Karbi Anglong and the Meghalaya Plateau in the northeast and Rajasthan in the west are also extensions of this block - The northeastern parts are separated by the Malda fault in West Bengal from the Chotanagpur plateau - In Rajasthan, the desert and other desert–like features overlay this block - Geological characteristics - The Peninsula is formed essentially by a great complex of very ancient gneisses and granites, which constitutes a major part of it - Since the Cambrian period, the Peninsula has been standing like a rigid block with the exception of some of its western coast which is submerged beneath the sea and some other parts changed due to tectonic activity without affecting the original basement - Tectonic activity - As a part of the Indo-Australian Plate, it has been subjected to various vertical movements and block faulting - The rift valleys of the Narmada, the Tapi and the Mahanadi and the Satpura block mountains are some examples of it - - - Topography and rivers - The Peninsula mostly consists of relict and residual mountains like the Aravali hills, the Nallamala hills, the Javadi hills, the Veliconda hills, the Palkonda range and the Mahendragiri hills, etc. - - The river valleys here are shallow with low gradients - Most of the east flowing rivers form deltas before entering into the Bay of Bengal - Why mostly eastern rivers and not western rivers? - Nature of eastern coastal plains - Broad and gently sloping plains reduce river velocity near the coast - Reduced velocity leads to heavy deposition of sediments - Sediment load of rivers - Large rivers like Ganga, Godavari, Krishna, and Mahanadi drain extensive catchments - They carry huge quantities of fine alluvium from the interior plateau and plains - Marine conditions of the Bay of Bengal - Weak tidal action and low wave energy as strong tides and currents prevent delta formation on west side - Deposited sediments are not removed or redistributed significantly - Peninsular plateu have a gentle slope from west to east - This prevents majority of the rivers from flowing westwards and also provide gentle sloping plains to form deltas - Coastal configuration - Eastern coast is largely a submerged coast - Submergence facilitates accumulation and spreading of sediments near river mouths - Why most of the rivers in the peninsular India flows from west to east? - General slope of the Peninsular Plateau - Plateau slopes gently from west to east - Western Ghats form a high escarpment on the western margin - Eastern Ghats are lower and discontinuous - Location of major watersheds - Main water divide lies close to the Western Ghats - Rivers originating near the Ghats have a longer eastward course - Structural stability of the Peninsular Block - Ancient and stable landmass - No major westward sloping trough across the plateau - Drainage evolution over geological time - Long-term erosion reinforced eastward drainage - Rivers adjusted to the regional slope - Yet there are exceptions - There are some rivers which flows from east to west, and mainly due to 2 reasons given below - Rivers originating on the Western Ghats - These arise on the western slope of the Western Ghats and flow directly into the Arabian Sea. - These rivers are generally: - Short - Swift - Seasonal - Form estuaries, not deltas - Examples (including the ones you listed): - Periyar - Bharathapuzha (Nila) - Mandovi - Zuari - Sharavathi - Kali - Netravati - Mahi (upper reaches near Vindhyas, but west-flowing) - Sabarmati (short west-flowing river) - Exception of river that does not originate from western ghats and still flow westward - Luni (due to Aravalli range) - Structurally controlled - These are structurally controlled rivers flowing through rift valleys. - These rivers have characteristics - Originate in central India (Amarkantak / Satpura region) - Flow westward due to faulting, not because of the Western Ghats - Much longer than typical west-coast rivers - Key examples: - Narmada - Tapi (Tapti) - As a result, extensive deltas are formed along the eastern coast of India - The deltas formed by the Mahanadi, the Krishna, the Kaveri and the Godavari are important examples 2. The Himalayas and other Peninsular Mountains - - Geological nature - The Himalayas are young, weak, and flexible fold mountains, whereas the Peninsular mountains are part of the ancient, rigid, and stable Peninsular Block. - Consequently, they are still subjected to the interplay of exogenic and endogenic forces, resulting in the development of faults, folds and thrust plains - What are the characteristics of old and youthful rivers? - Youthful rivers - Youthful rivers are those that flow through the young and tectonically active Himalayan region, which is still undergoing uplift. - Examples include Indus, Ganga, and Brahmaputra - Characteristics of youthful rivers: - They flow through the young and tectonically active Himalayan region, which is still undergoing uplift. - Continuous uplift increases the vertical erosion capacity of rivers, a key feature of the youthful stage. - Their courses are still adjusting to ongoing crustal movements, indicating geomorphic immaturity. - These rivers have: - Deep V-shaped valleys in their upper courses - Gorges and rapids - High erosive power - In youthful rivers, meanders are narrow and deeply cut into the land (incised meanders) because vertical erosion dominates. - Old rivers - Old rivers are those that flow over geologically stable regions and have reached an advanced stage of erosion. - Examples include Godavari, Krishna, Cauvery, Mahanadi, Narmada (mature to old in many stretches) - Characteristics of old rivers: - Flow over the Peninsular Plateau, which is ancient and stable - Dominated by lateral erosion - Wide floodplains - In mature and old rivers, meanders are wide, open, and clearly curved (pronounced meanders) because lateral erosion dominates. - Tectonic activity - These mountains are tectonic in origin, dissected by fast-flowing rivers which are in their youthful stage - Various landforms like gorges, V-shaped valleys, rapids, waterfalls, etc. are indicative of this stage 3. Indo-Ganga-Brahmaputra Plain - Geological extent and nature - The third geological division of India comprises the plains formed by the river Indus, the Ganga and the Brahmaputra - Originally, it was a geo-synclinal depression which attained its maximum development during the third phase of the Himalayan mountain formation approximately about 64 million years ago - Geo-synclinal depression is a structural depression caused by downward bending of the crust. The area keeps subsiding while sediments are deposited, allowing very thick sediment piles to form. - Since then, it has been gradually filled by the sediments brought by the Himalayan and Peninsular rivers - Average depth of alluvial deposits in these plains ranges from 1,000-2,000 m - Conclusion - It is evident from the above discussion that there are significant variations among the different regions of India in terms of their geological structure, which has far-reaching impact upon other related aspects - Variations in the physiography and relief are important among these - The relief and physiography of India has been greatly influenced by the geological and geomorphological processes active in the Indian subcontinent - Physiography - - - Definition and basics - Physiography refers to the study and description of the physical features of the Earth's surface. It includes aspects like: - Landforms (mountains, valleys, plateaus, plains) - Relief features - Drainage patterns - Soil types - Elevation and slope - ‘Physiography’ of an area is the outcome of structure, process and the stage of development - The land of India is characterised by great diversity in its physical features - The north has a vast expanse of rugged topography consisting of a series of mountain ranges with varied peaks, beautiful valleys and deep gorges - The south consists of stable table land with highly dissected plateaus, denuded rocks and developed series of scarps - In between these two lies the vast north Indian plain - Based on these macro variations, India can be divided into the following physiographic divisions: - 1. The Northern and Northeastern Mountains - - General features - The North and Northeastern Mountains consist of the Himalayas and the Northeastern hills - The Himalayas consist of a series of parallel mountain ranges - Some of the important ranges are the Greater Himalayan range, which includes the Great Himalayas and the Shiwalik - Orientation - The general orientation of these ranges is from northwest to the southeast direction in the northwestern part of India - Himalayas in the Darjiling and Sikkim regions lie in an eastwest direction, while in Arunachal Pradesh they are from southwest to the northwest direction (curved) - In Nagaland, Manipur and Mizoram, they are in the northsouth direction - Length - The approximate length of the Great Himalayan range, also known as the central axial range, is 2,500 km from east to west, and their width varies between 160-400 km from north to south - Barrier nature - It is also evident from the map that the Himalayas stand almost like a strong and long wall between the Indian subcontinent and the Central and East Asian countries - Himalayas are not only the physical barrier, they are also a climatic, drainage and cultural divide - Formation due to collision between Indian plate and Eurasian plate - - Range division of Himalyas - - The Himalayas are a vast mountain system that stretches across northern India and are classified into three major parallel ranges from north to south: - Greater Himalayas (Himadri): - This is the innermost and highest range, comprising some of the world’s tallest peaks like Mount Everest (Sagarmatha) and Kanchenjunga. - The range remains snow-covered throughout the year and is the source of many perennial rivers such as the Ganga, Yamuna, and Indus. - It is the most continuous of the three ranges and geologically the most ancient and stable. - Average height around 6000 meters (19,700 feet). - Lesser Himalayas (Himachal): - Situated south of the Himadri, this range is lower in elevation but is marked by rugged terrain and steep slopes. - It houses several important hill stations like Shimla, Mussoorie, and Nainital. - - Locally, this range is known as: - Dhauladhar in Himachal Pradesh - Nagtibha in Uttarakhand - Prominent sub-ranges include: - The Pir Panjal (longest and most important in lesser himalyas) - Mahabharat ranges. - It is moderately populated and agriculturally significant. - Average height between 3000 to 4500 meters (9,800 to 14,800 feet). - Shiwalik (Outer Himalayas): - These are the southernmost and lowest ranges, formed from the sediments (alluvium) brought down by the rivers from the upper Himalayas. - The term “Shiwalik” is derived from geological formations around a place named Sivawala near Dehra Dun, which was once a base of the Imperial Survey and later became the headquarters of the Geological Survey of India. - The region is characterized by frequent landslides and erosion due to its unconsolidated nature. - The area between the Shiwaliks and Lesser Himalayas is called the Duns (e.g., Dehra Dun, Patli Dun), known for their fertile soil and dense population. - Average height between 900 to 1500 meters (3,000 to 5,000 feet). - Trans-Himalyas - Trans-Himalayas refer to the mountain ranges located north of the Great Himalayan range and primarily within the territory of Tibet and parts of northern India like Ladakh. - The word "Trans" means beyond, so Trans-Himalayas = Beyond the Himalayas - The Trans-Himalayas act as a bridge between the Himalayas and the Tibetan Plateau - They are not part of the three main Himalayan ranges of the Indian Himalayan system due to: - Geological Formation: - Formed slightly earlier than the Himalayas. - Have a different tectonic and lithological structure. - Location: - Lie mostly in Tibet, Ladakh, and northernmost parts of India. - Located beyond the Indus River in many areas (hence also called Tibetan Himalayas sometimes). - Climate and Topography: - Drier, higher, and more barren than the main Himalayas. - Home to cold desert ecosystems. - Average height between 4500 to 6500 meters (14,800 to 21,300 feet). - Division from west to east - - Regional division of the Himalayas: - - - Kashmir or Northwestern Himalayas - - Ranges - It comprise a series of ranges such as the Karakoram, Ladakh, Zaskar and Pir Panjal - Climate - The northeastern part of the Kashmir Himalayas is a cold desert, which lies between the Greater Himalayas and the Karakoram ranges - Between the Great Himalayas and the Pir Panjal range, lies the world famous valley of Kashmir and the famous Dal Lake - The Kashmir Himalayas are also famous for Karewa formations, which are useful for the cultivation of Zafran, a local variety of saffron - Glaciers - Important glaciers of South Asia such as the Baltoro and Siachen are also found in this region - Passes - Some of the important passes of the region are Zoji La on the Great Himalayas, Banihal on the Pir Panjal, Photu La on the Zaskar and Khardung La on the Ladakh range - Lakes - Some of the important fresh lakes such as Dal and Wular and salt water lakes such as Pangong Tso and Tso Moriri are also in this region - Dal Lake in Srinagar presents an interesting physical feature - Rivers and drainage - This region is drained by the river Indus, and its tributaries such as the Jhelum and the Chenab - Srinagar, capital city of the union territory of Jammu and Kashmir is located on the banks of Jhelum river - Jhelum in the valley of Kashmir is still in its youth stage and yet forms meanders – a typical feature associated with the mature stage in the evolution of fluvial land form (Figure 2.4) - - In Kashmir Valley, the meanders in Jhelum river are caused by the local base level provided by the erstwhile larger lake of which the present Dal Lake is a small part - Karewas: - Karewas are the thick deposits of glacial clay and other materials embedded with moraines - Scenic beuty - The Kashmir and northwestern Himalayas are well-known for their scenic beauty and picturesque landscape - The landscape of Himalayas is a major source of attraction for adventure tourists - Some famous places of pilgrimage such as Vaishno Devi, Amarnath Cave, Charar -e-Sharif, etc. are also located here and large number of pilgrims visit these places every year - The southernmost part of this region consists of longitudinal valleys known as ‘duns’ - Jammu dun and Pathankot dun are important examples - The Himachal and Uttarakhand Himalayas - Location and features - This part lies approximately between the Ravi in the west and the Kali (a tributary of Ghaghara) in the east - The northernmost part of the Himachal Himalayas is an extension of the Ladakh cold desert, which lies in the Spiti subdivision of district Lahul and Spiti - All the three ranges of Himalayas are prominent in this section also - In this section of Lesser Himalayas, the altitude between 1,000-2,000 m specially attracted to the British colonial administration - Some of the important hill stations such as Dharamshala, Mussoorie, Shimla, Kaosani and the cantonment towns and health resorts such as Shimla, Mussoorie, Kasauli, Almora, Lansdowne and Ranikhet, etc. were developed in this region - The two distinguishing features of this region from the point of view of physiography are the ‘Shiwalik’ and ‘Dun formations’ - Some important duns located in this region are the Chandigarh-Kalka dun, Nalagarh dun, Dehra Dun, Harike dun and the Kota dun, etc. - Dehra Dun is the largest of all the duns with an approximate length of 35-45 km and a width of 22-25 km - River and drainage - It is drained by two major river systems of India, i.e. the Indus and the Ganga - Tributaries of the Indus include the river Ravi, the Beas and the Satluj, and the tributaries of Ganga flowing through this region include the Yamuna and the Ghaghara - Demographics - In the Great Himalayan range, the valleys are mostly inhabited by the Bhotia’s - These are nomadic groups who migrate to ‘Bugyals’ (the summer glasslands in the higher reaches) during summer months and return to the valleys during winters - Famous points - The famous ‘Valley of flowers’ is also situated in this region - The places of pilgrimage such as the Gangotri, Yamunotri, Kedarnath, Badrinath and Hemkund Sahib are also situated in this part - The region is also known to have five famous Prayags (river confluences) as mentioned in Chapter 3 of this book - The Darjiling and Sikkim Himalayas - Location and features - They are flanked by Nepal Himalayas in the west and Bhutan Himalayas in the east - It is relatively small but it is a significant part of the Himalayas - As compared to the other sections of the Himalayas, these along with the Arunachal Himalayas are conspicuous [clearly visible] by the absence of the Shiwalik formations - In place of the Shiwaliks here, the ‘duar formations’ are important, which have also been used for the development of tea gardens - Rivers and draingage - Known for its fast-flowing rivers such as Tista, it is a region of high mountain peaks like Kanchenjunga (Kanchengiri), and deep valleys - Demographics - The higher reaches of this region are inhabited by Lepcha tribes while the southern part, particularly the Darjiling Himalayas, has a mixed population of Nepalis, Bengalis and tribals from Central India - The British, taking advantage of the physical conditions such as moderate slope, thick soil cover with high organic content, well distributed rainfall throughout the year and mild winters, introduced tea plantations in this region - Scenic beuty - Sikkim and Darjiling Himalayas are also known for their scenic beauty and rich flora and fauna, particularly various types of orchids - The Arunachal Himalayas - Extent - These extend from the east of the Bhutan Himalayas up to the Diphu pass in the east - The general direction of the mountain range is from southwest to northeast - Features - Some of the important mountain peaks of the region are Kangtu and Namcha Barwa - These ranges are dissected [having a divided form or structure] by fast-flowing rivers from the north to the south, forming deep gorges - Rivers and drainage - Bhramaputra flows through a deep gorge after crossing Namcha Barwa - Some of the important rivers are the Kameng, the Subansiri, the Dibang and the Lohit - These are perennial [lasting or existing for a long or apparently infinite time] with the high rate of fall, thus, having the highest hydro-electric power potential in the country - Demographics - An important aspect of the Arunachal Himalayas is the numerous ethnic tribal community inhabiting in these areas - Some of the prominent ones from west to east are the Monpa, Abor, Mishmi, Nyishi and the Nagas - Most of these communities practise Jhumming - It is also known as shifting or slash and burn cultivation - Due to rugged topography, the inter-valley transportation linkages are nominal - Hence, most of the interactions are carried through the duar region along the Arunachal-Assam border - Biodiversity - This region is rich in biodiversity which has been preserved by the indigenous communities - The Eastern Hills and Mountains - - - Extend and location - These are part of the Himalayan mountain system having their general alignment from the north to the south direction - Names - They are known by different local names - In the north, they are known as Patkai Bum, Naga hills, the Manipur hills and in the south as Mizo or Lushai hills - Features - These are low hills, inhabited by numerous tribal groups practising Jhum cultivation - Most of these ranges are separated from each other by numerous small rivers - River and drainage - The Barak is an important river in Manipur and Mizoram - The physiography of Manipur is unique by the presence of a large lake known as ‘Loktak’ lake at the centre, surrounded by mountains from all sides - - Mizoram which is also known as the ‘Molassis basin’ which is made up of soft unconsolidated deposits - Most of the rivers in Nagaland form the tributary of the Brahmaputra - While two rivers of Mizoram and Manipur are the tributaries of the Barak river, which in turn is the tributary of Meghna; the rivers in the eastern part of Manipur are the tributaries of Chindwin, which in turn is a tributary of the Irrawady of Myanmar - Some major Himalyan glaciers in India - 2. The Northern Plains - - - Formation - The northern plains are formed by the alluvial deposits brought by the rivers – the Indus, the Ganga and the Brahmaputra - Features - These plains extend approximately 3,200 km from the east to the west - The average width of these plains varies between 150-300 km - The maximum depth of alluvium deposits varies between 1,000-2,000 m - From the north to the south, these can be divided into three major zones: - The Bhabar - Bhabar is a narrow belt ranging between 8-10 km parallel to the Shiwalik foothills at the break-up of the slope - As a result of this, the streams and rivers coming from the mountains deposit heavy materials of rocks and boulders, and at times, disappear in this zone - The Tarai - South of the Bhabar is the Tarai belt, with an approximate width of 10-20 km where most of the streams and rivers re-emerge without having any properly demarcated channel, thereby, creating marshy and swampy conditions known as the Tarai - This has a luxurious growth of natural vegetation and houses a varied wildlife - The south of Tarai is a belt consisting of old and new alluvial deposits known as the Bhangar (old) and Khadar (new) respectively - These plains have characteristic features of mature stage of fluvial erosional and depositional landforms such as sand bars, meanders, oxbow lakes and braided channels - The alluvial plains - The alluvial plains can be further divided into the Khadar (new) and the Bhangar (old) - States covered - Punjab, Haryana, Delhi, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, West Bengal, Assam, parts of Jharkhand. - Rivers and impact - The Brahmaputra plains are known for their riverine islands and sand bars - Most of these areas are subjected to periodic floods and shifting river courses forming braided streams - The mouths of these mighty rivers also form some of the largest deltas of the world, for example, the famous Sunderbans delta - Otherwise, this is a featureless plain with a general elevation of 50-150 m above the mean sea level - The states of Haryana and Delhi form a water divide between the Indus and the Ganga river systems - As opposed to this, the Brahmaputra river flows from the northeast to the southwest direction before it takes an almost 90° southward turn at Dhubri before it enters into Bangladesh - The Brahmaputra makes a spectacular Great Bend around Namcha Barwa in Tibet due to Himalayan tectonic uplift before entering India as the Dihang. - - These river valley plains have a fertile alluvial soil cover which supports a variety of crops like wheat, rice, sugarcane and jute, and hence, supports a large population - Division based on rivers - Punjab Plains (Indus system) - Ganga Plains (largest) - Brahmaputra Plains 3. The Peninsular Plateau - - Extent and Features - Rising from the height of 150 m above the river plains up to an elevation of 600-900 m is the irregular triangle known as the Peninsular plateau - Delhi ridge in the northwest, (extension of Aravalis), the Rajmahal hills in the east, Gir range in the west and the Cardamom hills in the south constitute the outer extent of the Peninsular plateau - However, an extension of this is also seen in the northeast, in the form of Shillong and Karbi-Anglong plateau - The Peninsular Plateau does not vanish; it submerges beneath the alluvium of the Indo-Gangetic Plain and then reappears in the north-east as detached blocks due to faulting and subsidence. - This is one of the oldest and the most stable landmass of India - The general elevation of the plateau is from the west to the east, which is also proved by the pattern of the flow of rivers - Patland plateaus - Patlands plateaus, or "high-level plateaus," are regional types of the Peninsular Plateau in India, specifically referring to lava-capped mesas with flat-topped surfaces. - These plateaus are characterized by their even surface and height, typically around 1000 meters (3300 ft) above ground level. Examples include the Chhotanagpur Plateau. - The Peninsular India is made up of a series of patland plateaus such as: - The Hazaribagh plateau - The Palamu plateau - The Ranchi plateau - The Malwa plateau - The Coimbatore plateau - The Karnataka plateau, - etc. - Physiographic features - Some of the important physiographic features of this region are tors, block mountains, rift valleys, spurs, bare rocky structures, series of hummocky hills and wall-like quartzite dykes offering natural sites for water storage - The western and northwestern part of the plateau has an emphatic presence of black soil - Past changes - This Peninsular plateau has undergone recurrent phases of upliftment and submergence accompanied by crustal faulting and fractures - The Bhima fault needs special mention, because of its recurrent seismic activities - Impact - These spatial variations have brought in elements of diversity in the relief of the Peninsular plateau - The northwestern part of the plateau has a complex relief of ravines and gorges - The ravines of Chambal, Bhind and Morena are some of the well-known examples - On the basis of the prominent relief features, the Peninsular plateau can be divided into three broad groups: - 1. The Deccan Plateau - Location and extent - This is bordered by the Western Ghats in the west, Eastern Ghats in the east and the Satpura, Maikal range and Mahadeo hills in the north - The Eastern and the Western Ghats meet each other at the Nilgiri hills - - Sub-division: - Western Ghats (Sahyadri Hills) - Western Ghats are locally known by different names such as Sahyadri in Maharashtra, Nilgiri hills in Karnataka and Tamil Nadu and Anaimalai hills and Cardamom hills in Kerala - Western Ghats are comparatively higher in elevation and more continuous than the Eastern Ghats - Their average elevation is about 1,500 m with the height increasing from north to south - ‘Anaimudi’ (2,695 m), the highest peak of Peninsular plateau is located on the Anaimalai hills of the Western Ghats followed by Dodabetta (2,637 m) on the Nilgiri hills - - Most of the Peninsular rivers have their origin in the Western Ghats - Western ghats runs parallel to the western coast from Maharashtra to Kerala. - Highest peaks reach around 2,695 m (Anaimudi in Kerala). - Rich in biodiversity and receives heavy monsoon rainfall. - Important for rivers like Godavari, Krishna, and Cauvery which originate here. - Eastern Ghats - Eastern Ghats comprising the discontinuous and low hills are highly eroded by the rivers such as the Mahanadi, the Godavari, the Krishna, the Kaveri, etc. - Some of the important ranges include the Javadi hills, the Palconda range, the Nallamala hills, the Mahendragiri hills, etc. - - Discontinuous range running from Odisha through Andhra Pradesh to Tamil Nadu. - Average height around 600–1,200 m. - Arma Konda (also known as Jindhagada Peak) is the highest peak approximately 1,690 meters (5,545 feet) - Mahendragiri (second highest peak) is often confused with highest peak and was traditionally considered the highest because of its mythological and cultural prominence (e.g., associated with Parashurama and Ramayana). - Less elevated and more eroded than Western Ghats. - Rivers like Mahanadi, Godavari, Krishna flow through gaps here. - Maharashtra Plateau - Part of the interior plateau in western Maharashtra. - Characterized by Deccan basaltic lava flows. - Average elevation around 600–900 m. - Karnataka Plateau - Located in the southern interior Deccan. - Includes regions of Mysore and Bangalore. - Composed mainly of volcanic basalt rock. - Average elevation about 600–900 m. - It is divided into two parts Maldan (hilly) and Maidan (plain) - Telangana Plateau - Lies between the Eastern and Western Ghats in Telangana and parts of Andhra Pradesh. - Features undulating terrain with some hill ranges. - Elevation varies around 300–600 m. - Malwa Plateau - Northern fringe of the Deccan Plateau in Madhya Pradesh. - Known for fertile black soils and agriculture. - Average height about 500–600 m. - Chhota Nagpur Plateau - Northeastern part, mainly Jharkhand. - Rich in minerals and forest cover. - Average height about 700–1,100 m. - Satpura Range - Runs east-west, south of the Narmada valley. - Acts as a watershed between Narmada and Tapi rivers. - Average elevation around 1,200 m. - Vindhya Range - Lies north of the Narmada valley. - Often considered the northern boundary of the Deccan Plateau. - Average elevation around 300–600 m. 2. The Central Highlands - Location and extent - They are bounded to the west by the Aravali range - The Satpura range is formed by a series of scarped plateaus on the south, generally at an elevation varying between 600-900 m above the mean sea level - - This forms the northernmost boundary of the Deccan plateau - It is a classic example of the relict mountains which are highly denuded and form discontinuous ranges - The extension of the Peninsular plateau can be seen as far as Jaisalmer in the West, where it has been covered by the longitudinal sand ridges and crescent-shaped sand dunes called barchans - An eastern extension of the Central Highland is formed by the Rajmahal hills, to the south of which lies a large reserve of mineral resources in the Chotanagpur plateau - Past events - This region has undergone metamorphic processes in its geological history, which can be corroborated [confirm or give support to] by the presence of metamorphic rocks such as marble, slate, gneiss, etc. - Features - The general elevation of the Central Highlands ranges between 700-1,000 m above the mean sea level and it slopes towards the north and northeastern directions - River and drainage - Most of the tributaries of the river Yamuna have their origin in the Vindhyan and Kaimur ranges - Banas is the only significant tributary of the river Chambal that originates from the Aravalli in the west 3. The Northeastern Plateau - Extend and location - In fact it is an extension of the main Peninsular plateau - An extension of this is also seen in the Karbi Anglong hills of Assam - Formation - It is believed that due to the force exerted by the northeastward movement of the Indian plate at the time of the Himalayan origin, a huge fault was created between the Rajmahal hills and the Meghalaya plateau - Later, this depression got filled up by the deposition activity of the numerous rivers - Today, the Meghalaya and Karbi Anglong plateau stand detached from the main Peninsular Block - The Meghalaya plateau is further sub-divided into three (named after the tribal groups inhabiting this region): 1. The Garo Hills 2. The Khasi Hills 3. The Jaintia Hills - - Mineral resources - Similar to the Chotanagpur plateau, the Meghalaya plateau is also rich in mineral resources like coal, iron ore, sillimanite, limestone and uranium - Rainfall and impact - This area receives maximum rainfall from the south west monsoon - As a result, the Meghalaya plateau has a highly eroded surface - Cherrapunji displays a bare rocky surface devoid of any permanent vegetation cover 4. The Indian Desert - - - Location and extend - To the northwest of the Aravali hills lies the Great Indian desert - Though the underlying rock structure of the desert is an extension of the Peninsular plateau, yet, due to extreme arid conditions, its surface features have been carved by physical weathering and wind actions - General features - It is a land of undulating [having a smoothly rising and falling form] topography dotted with longitudinal dunes and barchans - This region receives low rainfall below 150 mm per year; hence, it has arid climate with low vegetation cover - It is because of these characteristic features that this is also known as Marusthali - Some of the well pronounced desert land features present here are mushroom rocks, shifting dunes and oasis (mostly in its southern part) - Historical data - It is believed that during the Mesozoic era, this region was under the sea - This can be corroborated [confirm or give support to] by the evidence available at wood fossils park at Aakal and marine deposits around Brahmsar, near Jaisalmer (The approximate age of the woodfossils is estimated to be 180 million years) - On the basis of the orientation, the desert can be divided into two parts: a. The northern part is sloping towards Sindh b. The southern towards the Rann of Kachchh - River and drainage - Most of the rivers in this region are ephemeral [lasting for a very short time] - The Luni river flowing in the southern part of the desert is of some significance - Low precipitation and high evaporation makes it a water deficit region - There are some streams which disappear after flowing for some distance and present a typical case of inland drainage by joining a lake or playa - The lakes and the playas have brackish water which is the main source of obtaining salt 5. The Coastal Plains - - - Coastal plains are flat, low-lying land adjacent to a sea or ocean. They are formed by a combination of factors, including deposition of sediment by rivers and waves, and the slow subsidence or gradual lowering of the land. - India, with its extensive coastline, has distinct coastal plains on both its eastern and western sides. - On the basis of the location and active geomorphological processes, it can be broadly divided into two: 1. The western coastal plains - Extent and location - - Extending from the Gujarat coast in the north to the Kerala coast in the south, the western coast may be divided into following divisions - The Kachchh and Kathiawar coast in Gujarat - Konkan coast in Maharashtra and Goa - Malabar coast in Karnataka and Kerala - The Karnataka portion of the Malabar Coast is often called the Kannada (Canara) Coast. - The western coastal plains are narrow in the middle and get broader towards north and south - General features and extent - The western coastal plains are an example of submerged coastal plain - A submerging coast is one where sea level rises relative to the land or the land subsides, causing the sea to advance inland. - A submerging coast is formed when land subsides or sea level rises, resulting in drowned river valleys, estuaries, and backwaters - The rivers flowing through this coastal plain do not form any delta - The Malabar coast has got certain distinguishing features in the form of ‘Kayals’ (backwaters), which are used for fishing, inland navigation and also due to its special attraction for tourists - - Kayals are shallow lagoons or backwaters lying parallel to the coast. - They are formed where: - Sand bars / spits separate parts of the sea from the mainland. - Freshwater rivers mix with seawater, creating brackish conditions. - Vembanad Kayal located on the malabar coast of Kerala is the largest backwater in India - Historical data - It is believed that the city of Dwaraka which was once a part of the Indian mainland situated along the west coast is submerged under water - Because of this submergence it is a narrow belt and provides natural conditions for the development of ports and harbours - Ports - Kandla, Mazagaon, JLN port Navha Sheva, Marmagao, Mangalore, Cochin, etc. are some of the important natural ports located along the west coast - Human activity - Every year the famous Nehru Trophy Vallamkali (boat race) is held in Punnamada Kayal in Kerala 2. The eastern coastal plains - Extend and location - - As compared to the western coastal plain, the eastern coastal plain is broader and extending from West Bengal, covering Odisha, Andhra Pradesh to Tamil Nadu in the south. - Divided into the following parts: - Northern Circars - Extends from the Mahanadi delta to the Krishna delta - Covers coastal Odisha and Andhra Pradesh - Andhra Coast is the informal reference to Andhra Pradesh coastline - Coromandel Coast - Extends from the Krishna delta to Kanyakumari - Covers Tamil Nadu and southern Andhra Pradesh - Tamil coast is the informal reference to Tamil Nadu coastline - Features - Examples of emergent coast - An emerging coast is one where land rises relative to the sea or sea level falls, causing the coastline to advance seaward. - An emerging coast is formed when land uplift or sea-level fall exposes new coastal areas, leading to broad plains and delta formation. - There are well-developed deltas here, formed by the rivers flowing eastward in to the Bay of Bengal - These include the deltas of the Mahanadi, the Godavari, the Krishna and the Kaveri - Ports - Because of its emergent nature, it has less number of ports and harbours - Why submerged coastline make better natural ports? - Natural Sheltered Inlets (Indentations) - In submerged coastline drowning of existing river valleys and glacial troughs creates a highly irregular, indented coastline with numerous natural bays, coves, and deep-water inlets. - These indentations provide natural protection for ships against strong ocean currents, wind, and storms. - Rias (drowned river valleys) and Fjords (drowned glacial valleys) are classic submergent features that serve as deep, sheltered harbors. - Deep Water Near the Shore - A submerged coastline involves the sea flooding into valleys, the water near the shore is naturally deep. - In emerged coastline ships cannot get close to the shore without the risk of hitting the bottom. - Stability and Sedimentation - Emerged coastlines are often straight and smooth because they are basically former sea floors that have been pushed up. - Rivers flowing into these flat areas deposit massive amounts of sediment (silt and sand), creating deltas. This leads to constant silting, which requires expensive, continuous dredging to keep the port deep enough for ships. - Submerged Coasts often feature estuaries where the tide helps "flush" sediment out to sea, naturally keeping the harbor entrance clearer. - An estuary is a partially enclosed coastal body of water where freshwater from rivers mixes with salty ocean water, creating a unique, nutrient-rich environment called brackish water, which supports diverse ecosystems, provides nurseries for marine life, and serves as important transition zones between land and sea, often called bays, sounds, or lagoons - The continental shelf extends up to 500 km into the sea, which makes it difficult for the development of good ports and harbours - Chennai Port, Visakhapatnam Port, Paradip Port, Haldia Port, etc are some of the examples of ports in this region 6. The Islands - - - There are two major island groups in India - The Bay of Bengal island groups - Extend and location - The Bay of Bengal island groups consist of about 572 islands/islets [small island] - These are situated roughly between 6°N-14°N and 92°E-94°E - Principal Archipelagos: - Ritchie's Archipelago - Labyrinth Islands - The entire group of island is divided into two broad categories - - Categories a. The Andaman in the north b. The Nicobar in the south - They are separated by a waterbody which is called the Ten degree channel - It is called the "10-degree Channel" because its approximate latitude is at 10° N (10 degrees north of the Equator). - Size - The Andaman and Nicobar Islands together are about 5.5 times larger than Delhi (NCT). - Formation and features - It is believed that these islands are an elevated portion of submarine mountains - However, some smaller islands are volcanic in origin - Barren island, the only active volcano in India is also situated in the Nicobar islands - The coastal line has some coral deposits, and beautiful beaches - These islands receive convectional rainfall and have an equatorial type of vegetation - Past event - On 26 December 2004, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands were devastated by the Indian Ocean tsunami triggered by a massive undersea earthquake near Sumatra along the Sunda subduction zone. - Some important mountain peaks in Andaman and Nicobar Islands are: - Saddle peak (North Andaman – 738 m) - Mount Diavolo (Middle Andaman – 515 m) - Mount Koyob (South Andaman – 460 m) - Mount Thuiller (Great Nicobar – 642 m) - Wildlife - Hard to belive, but Andaman and Nicobar islands have elephants, which were introduced by the British during colonial era for manual work. - The Arabian Sea island groups - Location and extent - There are approximately 36 islands of which 11 are inhabited - These are scattered between 8°N-12°N and 71°E-74°E longitude - The key islands of the Arabian sea include Lakshadweep and Minicoy - These islands are located at a distance of 280 km-480 km off the Kerala coast - These islands combined are about 46 times smaller than Delhi (NCT). - Formation and features - The entire island group is built of coral deposits - Minicoy is the largest island with an area of 453 sq. km - The Islands of this archipelago have storm beaches consisting of unconsolidated pebbles, shingles, cobbles and boulders on the eastern seaboard - Drainage system - Introduction to drainage - - - - Definition - You have observed water flowing through the rivers, nalas and even channels during rainy season which drain the excess water - Had these channels not been there, large-scale flooding would have occurred - Wherever channels are ill-defined or choked, flooding is a common phenomenon - The flow of water through well-defined channels is known as ‘drainage’ and the network of such channels is called a ‘drainage system’ - The drainage pattern of an area is the outcome of the geological time period, nature and structure of rocks, topography, slope, amount of water flowing and the periodicity of the flow - Rivers flow in the same direction - Iit perennial [always with water] or ephemeral [water during rainy season, and dry, otherwise] - Key terms - Catchment area - Land area from which precipitation drains to a common outlet, catchment area is the umbrella area concept - Drainage basin and river basin are specific forms of catchment area - River-specific usage - River basin - Catchment area of a specific river system (main river + tributaries) - Used in basin-level planning, management, and administration - Drainage basin - Scientifically synonymous with catchment area - Emphasizes the drainage network rather than the river name - Watershed - Topographic divide separating two adjacent catchment areas; watershed is not an area, but a boundary. - It includes ridge, highland, or subtle elevation line - Important drainage patterns - - - Dendritic - The drainage pattern resembling the branches of a tree is known as “dendritic” the examples of which are the rivers of northern plain - Radial - When the rivers originate from a hill and flow in all directions, the drainage pattern is known as ‘radial’ - The rivers originating from the Amarkantak range present a good example of it - Trellis - When the primary tributaries of rivers flow parallel to each other and secondary tributaries join them at right angles, the pattern is known as ‘trellis’ - Centripetal - When the rivers discharge their waters from all directions in a lake or depression, the pattern is known as ‘centripetal’ - Significance - River basins and watersheds are marked by unity - What happens in one part of the basin or watershed directly affects the other parts and the unit as a whole - That is why, they are accepted as the most appropriate micro, meso or macro planning regions - Drainage systems of India - Overview - Indian drainage system consists of a large number of small and big rivers - It is the outcome of the evolutionary process of the three major physiographic units and the nature and characteristics of precipitation - Classification of Indian drainage system - On the basis of discharge orientation - Indian drainage system may be divided on various bases - On the basis of discharge of water (orientations to the sea), it may be grouped into: - The Arabian Sea drainage - Nearly 77 per cent of the drainage area consisting of the Ganga, the Brahmaputra, the Mahanadi, the Krishna, etc. is oriented towards the Bay of Bengal - The Bay of Bengal drainage - Approximately 23 per cent comprising the Indus, the Narmada, the Tapi, the Mahi and the Periyar systems discharge their waters in the Arabian Sea - They are separated from each other through the Delhi ridge, the Aravalis and the Sahyadris (water divide is shown by a line in Figure 3.1) - On the basis of on watershed size - Major - Major river basins with more than 20,000 sq. km of catchment area - It includes 14 drainage basins such as the Ganga, the Brahmaputra, the Krishna, the Tapi, the Narmada, the Mahi, the Pennar, the Sabarmati, the Barak, etc. (Appendix III) - Medium - Medium river basins with catchment area between 2,000-20,000 sq. km incorporating 44 river basins such as the Kalindi, the Periyar, the Meghna, etc. - Minor - Minor river basins with catchment area of less than 2,000 sq. km include fairly good number of rivers flowing in the area of low rainfall - On the basis of of origin and characteristics - Division - The Himalayan drainage - Rivers originate in the Himalayan mountain system, mostly perennial (fed by snowmelt + rainfall) - These have long courses, deep gorges in upper course, meanders, floodplains, oxbow lakes in plains - The Peninsular drainage - Rivers originate in the Peninsular Plateau, generally perennial, but with strong seasonal variation in discharge - These have shorter courses, shallow valleys, fixed, mature channels - The Chambal–Betwa–Son problem: - These rivers originate in the Vindhyan or Kaimur Hills, thus geologically part of the Peninsular plateau. - But they join the Ganga and are tributaries to a Himalayan system. - Hence, despite their Peninsular origin, they are hydrologically part of the Himalayan drainage. - This anomaly is accepted for practicality, and the classification sticks because it helps explain broader patterns of fluvial behavior. - Hence, this scheme has been followed in this book - Himanlayan and peninsular drainage indepth - The Himalayan drainage system - - Evolution of himalyan drainage - The Himalayan drainage system has evolved through a long geological history - There are difference of opinion about the evolution of the Himalayan rivers - However, geologists believe that a mighty river called Shiwalik or Indo-Brahma traversed the entire longitudinal extent of the Himalaya from Assam to Punjab and onwards to Sind, and finally discharged into the Gulf of Sind near lower Punjab during the Miocene period some 5-24 million years ago - The remarkable continuity of the Shiwalik and its lacustrine origin and alluvial deposits consisting of sands, silt, clay, boulders and conglomerates support this viewpoint - It is opined that in due course of time Indo–Brahma river was dismembered into three main drainage systems: 1. The Indus and its five tributaries in the western part 2. The Ganga and its Himalayan tributaries in the central part 3. The stretch of the Brahmaputra in Assam and its Himalayan tributaries in the eastern part - The dismemberment was probably due to the Pleistocene upheaval in the western Himalayas, including the uplift of the Potwar Plateau (Delhi Ridge), which acted as the water divide between the Indus and Ganga drainage systems - Likewise, the downthrusting of the Malda gap area between the Rajmahal hills and the Meghalaya plateau during the mid-pleistocene period, diverted the Ganga and the Brahmaputra systems to flow towards the Bay of Bengal - Rivers and their flow - It mainly includes the Ganga, the Indus and the Brahmaputra river basins - Since these are fed both by melting of snow and precipitation, rivers of this system are perennial - These rivers pass through the giant gorges carved out by the erosional activity carried on simultaneously with the uplift of the Himalayas - Besides deep gorges, these rivers also form V-shaped valleys, rapids and waterfalls in their mountainous course - While entering the plains, they form depositional features like flat valleys, ox-bow lakes, flood plains, braided channels, and deltas near the river mouth - In the Himalayan reaches, the course of these rivers is highly tortuous [twisting], but over the plains they display a strong meandering tendency and shift their courses frequently - River Kosi, also known as the ‘sorrow of Bihar’, has been notorious for frequently changing its course - The Kosi brings huge quantity of sediments from its upper reaches and deposits it in the plains - The course gets blocked, and consequently, the river changes its course - Major river systems of the Himalayan drainage - The Indus system - - Value addition - Origin Point: - Bokhar Chu Glacier, near Lake Mansarovar, Kailash Range, Tibet (4,164 m) - Path: - Tibet -> Ladakh (India) -> Gilgit-Baltistan (Pakistan) -> Punjab (Pakistan) -> Sindh (Pakistan) - Ending Point: - Arabian Sea, near Karachi, Pakistan (Indus Delta) - Tributaries: - Left Bank: - Himachal Pradesh -> Jammu and Kashmir -> Punjab (Pakistan) - Junction: - Near Panchnad, Pakistan - Feature: - World’s highest railway bridge (Chenab Bridge, 359 m) - Ravi: - Origin: - Bara Bhangal, Kangra, Himachal Pradesh - Junction: - Near Bannu, Pakistan - Feature: - Supports tribal region irrigation - Gomal: - Origin: - Sulaiman Range, Afghanistan - Path: - Afghanistan -> Khyber Pakhtunkhwa (Pakistan) - Junction: - Near Dera Ismail Khan, Pakistan - Feature: - Supports arid zone agriculture - Location, names and extend - It is one of the largest river basins of the world, covering an area of 11,65,000 sq. km (in India it is 321,289 sq. km and a total length of 2,880 km (in India 1,114 km)) - The Indus also known as the Sindhu, is the westernmost of the Himalayan rivers in India - It originates from a glacier near Bokhar Chu (31°15' N latitude and 81°40' E longitude) in the Tibetan region at an altitude of 4,164 m in the Kailash Mountain range newar mansarovar lake - In Tibet, it is known as ‘Singi Khamban; or Lion’s mouth - After flowing in the northwest direction between the Ladakh and Zaskar ranges, it passes through Ladakh and Baltistan - It cuts across the Ladakh range, forming a spectacular gorge near Gilgit in Jammu and Kashmir - It enters into Pakistan near Chilas in the Dardistan region - The Indus flows in India mainly through the Ladakh region (earlier part of Jammu and Kashmir) before entering Pakistan - Tributaries - The Indus receives a number of tributaries such as listed above. - It finally emerges out of the hills near Attock where it receives the Kabul river on its right bank - The river flows southward and receives ‘Panjnad’ a little above Mithankot - The Panjnad is the name given to the five rivers of Punjab, namely the Satluj, the Beas, the Ravi, the Chenab and the Jhelum - It finally discharges into the Arabian Sea, east of Karachi - Important tributories to indus - Jhelum - The Jhelum, an important tributary of the Indus, rises from a spring at Verinag situated at the foot of the Pir Panjal in the south-eastern part of the valley of Kashmir - It flows through Srinagar and the Wular lake before entering Pakistan through a deep narrow gorge - Wular lake is the largest freshwater lake in India. - Point of confusion - Vembanad lake in Kerala is the largest lake in India overall, but it’s brackish, not freshwater. - It joins the Chenab near Jhang in Pakistan - Chenab - The Chenab is the largest tributary of the Indus - It is formed by two streams, the Chandra and the Bhaga, which join at Tandi near Keylong in Himachal Pradesh - Hence, it is also known as Chandrabhaga - The river flows for 1,180 km before entering into Pakistan - Jhelum + Chenab alone contribute well over half of the Indus flow. - Ravi - The Ravi is another important tributary of the Indus - It rises west of the Rohtang pass in the Kullu hills of Himachal Pradesh and flows through the Chamba valley of the state - Before entering Pakistan and joining the Chenab near Sarai Sidhu, it drains the area lying between the southeastern part of the Pir Panjal and the Dhauladhar ranges - Ravi River is a tributary of the Chenab River; it flows into the Chenab in Pakistan, and then the combined waters join the Sutlej (which carries the Beas) to form the Panjnad, eventually emptying into the Indus. - Satluj - The Satluj originates in the ‘Raksas tal’ near Mansarovar at an altitude of 4,555 m in Tibet where it is known as Langchen Khambab - It is the longest of the five tributaries of the Indus. It receives the waters of the Beas first, and later the Chenab (which already carries the Jhelum and Ravi). - It flows almost parallel to the Indus for about 400 km before entering India, and comes out of a gorge at Rupar - It enters India through the Shipki La pass in the Kinnaur district of Himachal Pradesh. - It is an antecedent river - An antecedent river is a stream that maintains its original course and pattern despite changes in the underlying landscape, such as uplift or tectonic activity. - Essentially, the river existed before the landscape features that now form its path. - It is a very important tributary as it feeds the canal system of the Bhakra Nangal project - Beas - Originating from the Beas Kund near the Rohtang Pass at an elevation of 4,000 m above the mean sea level - It enters the Punjab plains where it meets the Satluj near Harike - It is a tributory to Sutluj - The river flows through the Kullu valley and forms gorges at Kati and Largi in the Dhaoladhar range - Among the traditional five rivers of Punjab, the merging occurs as follows: - 1. Jhelum and Ravi flow into the Chenab. 2. Beas flows into the Sutlej. 3. Chenab and Sutlej then merge to form the Panjnad River. 4. Panjnad finally joins the Indus. - The Ganga system - - - Value addition data - Origin Point: - Gangotri Glacier (Bhagirathi), Uttarakhand (4,100 m) - Path: - Uttarakhand -> Uttar Pradesh -> Bihar -> Jharkhand -> West Bengal -> Bangladesh - Location, names and extent - The Ganga is the most important river of India both from the point of view of its basin and cultural significance - It rises in the Gangotri glacier near Gaumukh (3,900 m) in the Uttarkashi district of Uttarakhand - Here, it is known as the Bhagirathi - It cuts through the Central and the Lesser Himalayas in narrow gorges - At Devprayag [prayag means where to rivers meet or sangam], the Bhagirathi meets the Alaknanda; hereafter, it is known as the Ganga - The Alaknanda has its source in the Satopanth glacier above Badrinath - The Alaknanda consists of the Dhauli and the Vishnu Ganga which meet at Vishnu Prayag near Joshimath - Joshimath is key spiritual center founded by Adi Shankaracharya and a base for tourists visiting Auli and Badrinath. - The other tributaries of Alaknanda such as the Pindar joins it at Karna Prayag while Mandakini or Kali Ganga meets it at Rudra Prayag - Do not confuse this "Kali Ganga" with the Kali River (also known as the Sharda), which forms the border between India and Nepal. - - The Ganga enters the plains at Haridwar - From here, it flows first to the south, then to the south-east and east before splitting into two distributaries, namely the Bhagirathi-Hooghly and the Padma - Bhagirathi-Hooghly: - The branch that flows south through West Bengal. - Padma: - The main branch that enters Bangladesh. - The river has a length of 2,525 km - It is shared by Uttarakhand (110 km) and Uttar Pradesh (1,450 km), Bihar (445 km) and West Bengal (520 km) - The Ganga basin covers about 8.6 lakh sq. km area in India alone - The Ganga river system is the largest in India having a number of perennial and non-perennial rivers originating in the Himalayas in the north and the Peninsula in the south, respectively - The river finally discharges itself into the Bay of Bengal near the Sagar Island - Important tributaries - The important left bank tributaries - Ramganga - The Ramganga is comparatively a small river rising in the Garhwal hills near Gairsain - It changes its course to the southwest direction after crossing the Shiwalik and enters into the plains of Uttar Pradesh near Najibabad - Finally, it joins the Ganga near Kannauj - The Gomati - The Gomati originates from Pilibhit district in Uttar Pradesh, near the Madhav Tanda area. - It flows entirely through Uttar Pradesh. - Major cities/towns on its banks include Lucknow, Jaunpur, and Sultanpur. - It travels roughly 900 km before meeting the Ganga. - Ghaghara - The Ghaghara originates in the glaciers of Mapchachungo - After collecting the waters of its tributaries – Tila, Seti and Beri, it comes out of the mountain, cutting a deep gorge at Shishapani - Subtributory - Sarda (Kali/Chauk river) - The Sarda or Saryu river rises in the Milam glacier in the Nepal Himalayas where it is known as the Goriganga - Along the Indo-Nepal border, it is called Kali or Chauk, where it joins the Ghaghara - Gandak - The Gandak comprises two streams, namely Kaligandak and Trishulganga - It rises in the Nepal Himalayas between the Dhaulagiri and Mount Everest and drains the central part of Nepal - It enters the Ganga plain in Champaran district of Bihar and joins the Ganga at Sonpur near Patna - Burhi Gandak - Originates from western slopes of sumesar hills near the India-Nepal border. - It joins the ganga near Monghyr town. - Kosi - The Kosi is an antecedent river with its source to the north of Mount Everest in Tibet, where its main stream Arun rises - Subtributory - Son Kosi and Tamur Kosi - After crossing the Central Himalayas in Nepal, it is joined by the Son Kosi from the West and the Tamur Kosi from the east - It forms Sapt Kosi after uniting with the river Arun - The Kosi River is known as the "Sorrow of Bihar" due to its frequent and damaging floods in the region - Mahananda - The Mahananda is another important tributary of the Ganga rising in the Darjiling hills - It joins the Ganga as its last left bank tributary in West Bengal - Important right bank tributories - Son - The Son is its major right bank tributary - The Son is a large south bank tributary of the Ganga, originating in the Amarkantak plateau - After forming a series of waterfalls at the edge of the plateau, it reaches Arrah, west of Patna, to join the Ganga - Yamuna - The Yamuna, the western most and the longest tributary of the Ganga, has its source in the Yamunotri glacier on the western slopes of Banderpunch range - It joins the Ganga at Prayag (Allahabad) - It is joined by (on its right bank which originates from the Peninsular plateau): - The Chambal - The Chambal rises near Mhow in the Malwa plateau of Madhya Pradesh and flows northwards through a gorge up wards of Kota in Rajasthan, where the Gandhisagar dam has been constructed - From Kota, it traverses down to Bundi, Sawai Madhopur and Dholpur, and finally joins the Yamuna - The Chambal is famous for its badland topography called the Chambal ravines - The Sind - The Betwa - The Ken - It is joined by (join it on its left bank): - The Hindan - The Rind - The Sengar - The Varuna, etc. - Much of its water feeds the western and eastern Yamuna and the Agra canals for irrigation purposes - Case about Damodar - The Damodar River is a tributary of the Hugli River which is a distributary of the Ganga. - The Damodar occupies the eastern margins of the Chotanagpur Plateau where it flows through a rift valley and finally joins the Hugli - The Barakar is its main tributary - Once known as the ‘sorrow of Bengal’, the Damodar has been now tamed by the Damodar Valley corporation, a multipurpose project - Government policy - ‘Namami Gange Programme’, is an Integrated Conservation Mission, approved as “Flagship Programme” by the Union Government in June 2014 with the twin objectives of effective abatement of pollution, conservation and rejuvenation of the National River Ganga - Main pillars of the Namami Gange Programme are: - Sewerage Treatment Infrastructure - River-Front Development - River-Surface Cleaning - Bio-Diversity - Afforestation - Public Awareness - Industrial Effluent Monitoring - Ganga Gram - The Brahmaputra system - - - Value addition data - Origin Point: - Angsi Glacier, Kailash Range, Tibet (Yarlung Tsangpo) - Path: - Tibet -> Arunachal Pradesh -> Assam -> Bangladesh - Ending Point: - Burhi Dihing: - Origin: - Patkai Hills, Arunachal Pradesh - Path: - Arunachal Pradesh -> Assam - Junction: - Near Khowang, Assam - Feature: - Supports Assam’s tea estates - Dhansiri: - Origin: - Laisang Peak, Nagaland - Path: - Nagaland -> Assam - Junction: - Near Numaligarh, Assam - Feature: - Supports Kaziranga National Park - Kolong: - Origin: - Naga Hills, Assam - Path: - Assam - Junction: - Near Nagaon, Assam - Junction: - Near Jogighopa, Assam - Feature: - UNESCO site, Manas National Park - Beki: - Origin: - Bhutan Himalayas - Path: - Bhutan -> Assam - Junction: - Near Barpeta, Assam - Feature: - Supports Barpeta’s agriculture - Teesta: - Origin: - Pauhunri Glacier, Sikkim - Path: - Sikkim -> West Bengal -> Bangladesh - Junction: - Near Jalpaiguri, West Bengal - Feature: - Major hydropower source in Sikkim - Location, name and extent - The Brahmaputra, one of the largest rivers of the world, has its origin in the Chemayungdung glacier of the Kailash range near the Mansarovar lake - From here, it traverses eastward longitudinally for a distance of nearly 1,200 km in a dry and flat region of southern Tibet, where it is known as the Tsangpo, which means ‘the purifier’ - It emerges as a turbulent and dynamic river after carving out a deep gorge in the Central Himalayas near Namcha Barwa (7,755 m) - It is one of the deepest and longest canyons in the world—even deeper than the Grand Canyon in the United States. - This forces the river to take a sharp, U-turn-like "S-bend" to the south. This is known as a syntaxial bend in geological terms. - The river emerges from the foothills under the name of Siang or Dihang - It enters India west of Sadiya town in Arunachal Pradesh - Flowing southwest, it receives its main left bank tributaries, viz., Dibang or Sikang and Lohit; thereafter, it is known as the Brahmaputra - The Brahmaputra receives numerous tributaries in its 750 km long journey through the Assam valley - The Brahmaputra enters into Bangladesh near Dhubri and flows southward - It finally merges with the river Padma - The combined flow of the Padma and Jamuna eventually joins the Meghna River before emptying into the Bay of Bengal through a vast delta. - Important tributories - Its major left bank tributaries are: - The Burhi Dihing - Dhansari (South) - Important right bank tributaries are: - Rango Tsango - The Rango Tsangpo is the major right bank tributary of this river in Tibet - The Subansiri - The Subansiri which has its origin in Tibet, is an antecedent river - Kameng (Jia Bhareli) - Manas - Sankosh - Tista - In Bangladesh, the Tista joins it on its right bank from where the river is known as the Jamuna, Jamuna is the name of the Brahmaputra in Bangladesh - Features - The Brahmaputra is well-known for floods, channel shifting and bank erosion - This is due to the fact that most of its tributaries are large, and bring large quantity of sediments owing to heavy rainfall in its catchment area - The Peninsular drainage system - - Evolution of peninsular drainage system - The Peninsular drainage system is older than the Himalayan one - This is evident from the broad, largely-graded shallow valleys, and the maturity of the rivers - Extent and rivers - The Western Ghats running close to the western coast act as the water divide between the major Peninsular rivers, discharging their water in the Bay of Bengal and as small rivulets joining the Arabian Sea - Most of the major Peninsular rivers except Narmada and Tapi flow from west to east - The Chambal, the Sind, the Betwa, the Ken, the Son, originating in the northern part of the Peninsula belong to the Ganga river system - The other major river systems of the Peninsular drainage are – the Mahanadi, the Godavari, the Krishna and the Kaveri - Peninsular rivers are characterised by fixed course, absence of meanders and nonperennial flow of water - The Narmada and the Tapi which flow through the rift valley are, however, exceptions - The evolution of Peninsular drainage system - Three major geological events in the distant past have shaped the present drainage systems of Peninsular India: 1. Subsidence [gradual sinking] of the western flank of the Peninsula leading to its submergence below the sea during the early tertiary period - Generally, it has disturbed the symmetrical plan of the river on either side of the original watershed 2. Upheaval of the Himalayas when the northern flank of the Peninsular block was subjected to subsidence and the consequent trough faulting - The Narmada and The Tapi flow in trough faults and fill the original cracks with their detritus materials - Hence, there is a lack of alluvial and deltaic deposits in these rivers 3. Slight tilting of the Peninsular block from northwest to the southeastern direction gave orientation to the entire drainage system towards the Bay of Bengal during the same period - River systems of the Peninsular drainage - Maan - Uri (Uriya) - Hatni - Orsang - The Narmada originates on the western flank of the Amarkantak plateau at a height of about 1,057 m - Flowing in a rift valley between the Satpura in the south and the Vindhyan range in the north, it forms a picturesque gorge in marble rocks and Dhuandhar waterfall near Jabalpur - After flowing a distance of about 1,312 km, it meets the Arabian sea south of Bharuch, forming a broad 27 km long estuary - It flows from Madhya Pradesh -> Maharashtra -> Gujarat - Its catchment area is about 98,796 sq. km - The Sardar Sarovar Project has been constructed on this river - Tapi - - - Value addition data - Origin Point: - Satpura Range, Betul, Madhya Pradesh - Path: - Madhya Pradesh -> Maharashtra -> Gujarat - Feature: - Supports Bharuch’s agriculture - The Tapi is the other important westward flowing river - It originates from Multai in the Betul district of Madhya Pradesh - It is 724 km long and drains an area of 65,145 sq. km -
