- Natural disasters and hazards in India - It was discussed in one of the previous chapters that India is vast and diverse in terms of its physical and socio-cultural attributes - It is largely due to its vast geographical area, environmental diversities and cultural pluralities that scholars often described it using two meaningful adjectives like the ‘Indian-subcontinent’ and the ‘land of unity in diversity’ - Its vastness in terms of natural attributes combined with its prolonged colonial past, continuing various forms of social discriminations and also equally large population have enhanced its vulnerability to natural disasters - These observations can also be illustrated by focussing on some of the major natural disasters in India - Earthquakes - - - Nature and causes - Earthquakes are by far the most unpredictable and highly destructive of all the natural disasters - You have already learnt the causes of earthquakes in your book Fundamentals of Physical Geography (NCERT, 2006) - Earthquakes that are of tectonic origin have proved to be the most devastating and their area of influence is also quite large - These earthquakes result from a series of earth movements brought about by a sudden release of energy during the tectonic activities in the earth’s crust - As compared to these, the earthquakes associated with volcanic eruption, rock fall, landslides, subsidence, particularly in the mining areas, impounding of dams and reservoirs, etc. have limited area of influence and the scale of damage - Distribution in India - It was mentioned in Chapter 2 of the book that the Indian plate is moving at a speed of one centimetre per year towards the north and northeastern direction and this movement of plates is being constantly obstructed by the Eurasian plate from the north - As a result of this, both the plates are said to be locked with each other resulting in accumulation of energy at different points of time - Excessive accumulation of energy results in building up of stress, which ultimately leads to the breaking up of the lock and the sudden release of energy causes earthquakes along the Himalayan arch - Some of the most vulnerable union territories/states are Jammu and Kashmir, Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, Sikkim, and the Darjeeling subdivision of West Bengal, and all the seven states of the northeast - Apart from these regions, the central-western parts of India, particularly Gujarat (in 1819, 1956 and 2001) and Maharashtra (in 1967 and 1993) have also experienced some severe earthquakes - Earth scientists have found it difficult to explain the occurrence of earthquakes in one of the oldest, most stable and mature landmass of Peninsular block for a long time - Recently, some earth scientists have come up with a theory of emergence of a fault line and energy build-up along the fault line represented by the river Bhima (Krishna) near Latur and Osmanabad (Maharashtra) and the possible breaking down of the Indian plate (Figure 7.2) - Earthquake zones - National Geophysical Laboratory, Geological Survey of India, Department of Meteorology, Government of India, along with the recently formed National Institute of Disaster Management, have made an intensive analysis of more than 1,200 earthquakes that have occurred in India in different years in the past - Based on these, they divided India into the following five earthquake zones: 1. Very high damage risk zone 2. High damage risk zone 3. Moderate damage risk zone 4. Low damage risk zone 5. Very low damage risk zone - Out of these, the first two zones had experienced some of the most devastating earthquakes in India - As shown in the Figure 7.2, areas vulnerable to these earthquakes are the North-east states, areas to the north of Darbhanga and Araria along the Indo-Nepal border in Bihar, Uttarakhand, Western Himachal Pradesh (around Dharamshala) and Kashmir Valley in the Himalayan region and the Kuchchh (Gujarat) - These are included in the Very High Damage Risk Zone - Similarly, the remaining parts of Jammu and Kashmir, Ladakh, Himachal Pradesh, Northern parts of Punjab, Eastern parts of Haryana, Delhi, Western Uttar Pradesh, and Northern Bihar fall under the High Damage Risk Zone - Remaining parts of the country fall under moderate to very Low Damage Risk Zone - Most of the areas that can be considered safe are from the stable landmass covered under the Deccan plateau - Socio-environmental consequences of earthquakes - The idea of an earthquake is often associated with fear and horror due to the scale, magnitude and suddenness at which it spreads disasters on the surface of the earth without discrimination - It becomes a calamity when it strikes the areas of high density of population - It not only damages and destroys the settlements, infrastructure, transport and communication network, industries and other developmental activities but also robs the population of their material and socio-cultural gains that they have preserved over generations - It renders them homeless, which puts an extra-pressure and stress, particularly on the weak economy of the developing countries - Effects of earthquakes -
On Ground (Geological Effects) On Manmade Structures (Built Environment) On Water Bodies (Hydrological Effects)
  • Surface Ruptures: Visible cracks and fissures along fault lines
  • Ground Shaking: Vibration of Earth's crust causing instability
  • Soil Liquefaction: Saturated soil loses strength and behaves like a liquid
  • Landslides: Triggered in hilly terrain due to slope failure
  • Land Subsidence or Uplift: Vertical displacement of ground surface
  • Structural Cracks: In walls, foundations, bridges, etc.
  • Collapse of Buildings: Especially non-engineered constructions
  • Tilting or Overturning: Of high-rise buildings and monuments
  • Buckling of Roads and Railways: Disrupts transportation
  • Damage to Infrastructure: Power lines, dams, pipelines
  • Seiches: Oscillating waves in enclosed water bodies like lakes
  • Hydrodynamic Pressure: Impacts dams and embankments
  • Tsunami: Massive sea waves generated due to underwater quakes
  • Flooding: Due to breach of water-retaining structures
  • Chain Reactions: Water system failures can trigger secondary hazards
- Apart from these, earthquakes also have some serious and far-reaching environmental consequences - Surface seismic waves produce fissures on the upper layers of the earth’s crust through which water and other volatile materials gush out, inundating the neighbouring areas - Earthquakes are also responsible for landslides and often these cause obstructions in the flow of rivers and channels resulting in the formation of reservoirs - Sometimes, rivers also change their course causing floods and other calamities in the affected areas - Famous examples - Global -
Earthquake Year Location Magnitude Impact
Great Chilean Earthquake 1960 Chile 9.5 Strongest ever recorded; massive tsunamis across the Pacific
Sumatra-Andaman (Indian Ocean) 2004 Indonesia 9.1–9.3 Devastating tsunami; ~230,000 deaths across 14 countries
Tohoku Earthquake 2011 Japan 9.0 Triggered Fukushima nuclear disaster; ~16,000 deaths
Haiti Earthquake 2010 Haiti 7.0 Over 200,000 deaths; massive humanitarian crisis
San Francisco Earthquake 1906 USA ~7.9 Urban destruction; widespread fires; ~3,000 deaths
Nepal Earthquake 2015 Nepal 7.8 ~9,000 deaths; major destruction in Kathmandu valley and Everest region
National
Bhuj Earthquake 2001 Gujarat 7.7 ~20,000 deaths; widespread damage in Kutch region
Latur Earthquake 1993 Maharashtra 6.3 ~10,000 deaths; unexpected in stable Deccan plateau
Assam Earthquake 1950 Arunachal & Assam 8.6 One of the largest in India; triggered floods & landslides
Kangra Earthquake 1905 Himachal Pradesh 7.8 ~20,000 deaths; major structural damage
Uttarkashi Earthquake 1991 Uttarakhand 6.8 ~1,000 deaths; exposed Himalayan region's vulnerability
- Earthquake hazard mitigation [the action of reducing the severity, seriousness, or painfulness of something] - Unlike other disasters, the damages caused by earthquakes are more devastating - Since it also destroys most of the transport and communication links, providing timely relief to the victims becomes difficult - It is not possible to prevent the occurrence of an earthquake; hence, the next best option is to emphasis on disaster preparedness and mitigation rather than curative measures such as: - Structural Measures: - Construction of earthquake-resistant buildings using seismic-safe designs and materials. - Retrofitting and strengthening of old or vulnerable buildings, bridges, and infrastructure. - Use of base isolators, shock absorbers, and cross-bracing in high-risk zones. - Implementation of building codes (e.g., IS 1893, IS 4326 in India) in urban and rural construction. - Non-Structural Measures: - Establishing earthquake monitoring centres (seismological centres) for regular monitoring and fast dissemination of information among the people in the vulnerable areas - Use of Geographical Positioning System (GPS) can be of great help in monitoring the movement of tectonic plates - Preparing a vulnerability map of the country and dissemination of vulnerability risk information among the people and educating them about the ways and means minimising the adverse impacts of disasters - Proper land-use planning: Avoiding settlements on fault lines or soft soils. - Creating detailed seismic hazard maps to guide planning and construction. - Strict enforcement of zoning laws and earthquake-safe construction norms. - Community Preparedness: - Public awareness campaigns on how to respond before, during, and after earthquakes. - Regular mock drills and training for schools, offices, and residential areas. - Identification and display of safe spots, emergency exits, and assembly points. - Emergency Response: - Quick mobilization of search and rescue teams with trained personnel and equipment. - Setup of temporary shelters, food, water, and emergency healthcare services. - Rapid damage assessment and coordination among emergency services. - Institutional Measures: - Role of National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) in policy and planning. - Integration of seismic risk into city master plans and Smart City projects. - Coordination with IMD and Geological Survey of India for real-time alerts. - Long-Term Strategies: - Promoting earthquake education at school and community levels. - Development of early warning systems (under research in some countries). - Investment in seismic monitoring networks and disaster-resilient infrastructure. - Tsunami - - Nature and causes - Earthquakes and volcanic eruptions that cause the sea-floor to move abruptly resulting in sudden displacement of ocean water in the form of high vertical waves are called tsunamis (harbour waves) or seismic sea waves - Normally, the seismic waves cause only one instantaneous vertical wave; but, after the initial disturbance, a series of afterwaves are created in the water that oscillate between high crest and low trough in order to restore the water level - Characteristics - The speed of wave in the ocean depends upon the depth of water - It is more in the shallow water than in the ocean deep - As a result of this, the impact of tsunami is less over the ocean and more near the coast where they cause large-scale devastations - Therefore, a ship at sea is not much affected by tsunami and it is difficult to detect a tsunami in the deeper parts of sea - It is so because over deep water the tsunami has very long wave-length and limited wave-height - Thus, a tsunami wave raises the ship only a metre or two and each rise and fall takes several minutes - As opposed to this, when a tsunami enters shallow water, its wave-length gets reduced and the period remains unchanged, which increases the wave-height - Sometimes, this height can be up to 15m or more, which causes large-scale destructions along the shores - Thus, these are also called Shallow Water Waves - Distribution - Tsunamis are frequently observed along the Pacific ring of fire, particularly along the coast of Alaska, Japan, Philippines, and other islands of Southeast Asia, Indonesia, Malaysia, Myanmar, Sri Lanka, and India etc. - Consequences - After reaching the coast, the tsunami waves release enormous energy stored in them and water flows turbulently onto the land destroying port-cities and towns, structures, buildings and other settlements - Since the coastal areas are densely populated the world over, and these are also centres of intense human activity, the loss of life and property is likely to be much higher by a tsunami as compared to other natural hazards in the coastal areas - The extent of devastation caused by tsunami can be assessed through the visuals on Banda Ache (Indonesia) presented in the book Practical Work in Geography - Part I (NCERT, 2006) - Famous examples -
Tsunami Year Location Cause Impact
Indian Ocean Tsunami 2004 Indonesia, Sri Lanka, India, Thailand, & others Undersea megathrust earthquake (Magnitude 9.1–9.3) ~230,000 deaths; widespread destruction across 14 countries -
Tohoku Tsunami 2011 Japan Magnitude 9.0 earthquake off the coast of Japan ~16,000 deaths; Fukushima nuclear disaster triggered
Lisbon Tsunami 1755 Portugal Earthquake-triggered tsunami Thousands killed; major destruction in Lisbon and coastal areas
Alaska Tsunami 1964 Alaska, USA Magnitude 9.2 earthquake 131 deaths; massive coastal damage and waves across the Pacific
Chile Tsunami 1960 Chile Great Chilean earthquake (Magnitude 9.5) Strongest earthquake recorded; tsunamis reached as far as Hawaii & Japan
Krakatoa Tsunami 1883 Indonesia (Sunda Strait) Volcanic eruption 36,000+ deaths; huge waves and global climatic impact
- Mitigation [the action of reducing the severity, seriousness, or painfulness of something] - Unlike other natural hazards, the mitigation of hazards created by tsunami is difficult, mainly because of the fact that losses are on a much larger scale - It is beyond the capacity of individual state or government to mitigate the damage - Structural Measures: - Construction of sea walls, breakwaters, and tsunami-resistant buildings in coastal areas. - Elevation of critical infrastructure (e.g., hospitals, shelters) above predicted inundation levels. - Establishment of green belts (mangroves, casuarina trees) along coastlines to act as natural barriers. - Development of elevated evacuation routes and safe shelters inland. - Non-Structural Measures: - Installation of early warning systems using seismic sensors and tide gauges. - Real-time dissemination of warnings through sirens, mobile alerts, radio, and TV. - Preparation and regular updating of tsunami hazard and vulnerability maps. - Enforcing coastal zone regulation (CZR) to prevent unplanned development in high-risk areas. - Community Preparedness: - Awareness campaigns and school-based education programs on tsunami safety. - Regular evacuation drills and training of local disaster response teams. - Public display of tsunami evacuation routes and safe zones. - Emergency Response: - Immediate evacuation of coastal populations upon receiving warnings. - Provision of temporary shelters, food, water, and medical aid. - Rapid deployment of search and rescue teams and damage assessment units. - Institutional Measures: - India has volunteered to join the International Tsunami Warning System after the December 2004 tsunami disaster - Role of Indian National Centre for Ocean Information Services (INCOIS) in tsunami forecasting. - Coordination with international tsunami warning networks (e.g., Pacific Tsunami Warning Center). - Integration of tsunami risk in district and state disaster management plans. - Implementation of the Disaster Management Act, 2005. - Long-Term Strategies: - Sustainable coastal development and protection of natural coastal ecosystems. - Research and investment in better prediction technologies. - Inclusion of tsunami risk in urban planning and infrastructure design. - Tropical cyclone - - Nature and causes - Tropical cyclones are intense low-pressure areas confined to the area lying between 30° N and 30° S latitudes, in the atmosphere around which high velocity winds blow - Disasters due to cyclones, unlike the ones caused by earthquakes, tsunamis and volcanic eruptions are more predictable in terms of the time and place of their occurrences - Horizontally, it extends up to 500-1,000 km and vertically from surface to 12-14 km - A tropical cyclone or hurricane is like a heat engine that is energised by the release of latent heat on account of the condensation of moisture that the wind gathers after moving over the oceans and seas - A heat engine is a system that converts heat energy into mechanical energy or work. Similarly, a tropical cyclone operates by converting the energy from warm ocean water into the wind and storm system. - Latent heat is the energy released when water vapor condenses into liquid water — this happens when moist air rises and cools in the atmosphere. - As winds blow over warm ocean waters, they pick up moisture. When this moist air rises (due to convection), it cools and water vapor condenses into clouds and rain. This condensation releases latent heat, which warms the surrounding air, causing it to rise further and intensify the cyclone. - The ocean surface provides the heat and moisture needed for cyclone formation. This is why tropical cyclones form over warm ocean waters (typically above 26.5°C). - There are differences of opinion among scientists about the exact mechanism of a tropical cyclone - However, some initial conditions for the emergence of a tropical cyclone are: 1. Large and continuous supply of warm and moist air that can release enormous latent heat 2. Strong Coriolis force that can prevent filling of low pressure at the centre (absence of Coriolis force near the equator prohibits the formation of tropical cyclone between 0°-5° latitude) 3. Unstable condition through the troposphere that creates local disturbances around which a cyclone develops 4. Finally, absence of strong vertical wind wedge, which disturbs the vertical transport of latent heat - Structure of tropical cyclone - Tropical cyclones are characterised by large pressure gradients - The centre of the cyclone is mostly a warm and low-pressure, cloudless core known as eye of the storm - Generally, the isobars are closely placed to each other showing high-pressure gradients - Normally, it varies between 14-17mb/100 km, but sometimes it can be as high as 60mb/100km - Expansion of the wind belt is about 10-150 km from the centre - Spatio-temporal distribution of tropical cyclone in India - Owing to its Peninsular shape surrounded by the Bay of Bengal in the east and the Arabian Sea in the west, the tropical cyclones in India also originate in these two important locations - Though most of the cyclones originate between 10°-15° north latitudes during the monsoon season, yet in case of the Bay of Bengal, cyclones mostly develop during the months of October and November - Here, they originate between 16°-2° N latitudes and to the west of 92° E - By July the place of origin of these storms shifts to around 18° N latitude and west of 90°E near the Sunderban Delta - Consequences of tropical cyclones - It was mentioned that the energy to the tropical cyclone comes from the latent heat released by the warm moist air - Hence, with the increase in distance from the sea, the force of the cyclone decreases - In India, the force of the cyclone decreases with increase in distance from the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea - So, the coastal areas are often struck by severe cyclonic storms with an average velocity of 180 km/h - Often, this results in abnormal rise in the sea level known as Storm Surge - A surge is generated due to interaction of air, sea and land - The cyclone provides the driving force in the form of very high horizontal pressure-gradient and very strong surface winds - The sea water flows across the coast along with strong winds and heavy downpour - This results in inundation [flooding] of human settlements, agricultural fields, damaging crops and destruction of structures created by human beings - Famous examples -
Tropical Cyclone Year Location Category / Intensity Impact
Bhola Cyclone 1970 Bangladesh (then East Pakistan) Category 3 (Estimated) ~500,000 deaths; one of the deadliest cyclones in history
Super Cyclone Odisha 1999 Odisha, India Category 5 ~10,000 deaths; massive destruction in coastal Odisha
Hurricane Katrina 2005 USA (Gulf Coast) Category 5 (at peak) 1,800+ deaths; major flooding and damage in New Orleans
Super Cyclone Amphan 2020 West Bengal, Odisha, Bangladesh Category 5 (Very Severe Cyclonic Storm) ~100 deaths; widespread damage to infrastructure and agriculture
Hurricane Harvey 2017 USA (Texas) Category 4 Severe flooding; over $125 billion in damages
Typhoon Haiyan 2013 Philippines Category 5 6,300+ deaths; massive destruction in Eastern Philippines
- Mitigation [the action of reducing the severity, seriousness, or painfulness of something] - Structural Measures: - Construction of cyclone-resistant buildings and infrastructure. - Building of cyclone shelters in vulnerable coastal areas. - Construction and strengthening of embankments, sea walls, and dykes to reduce storm surge impact. - Afforestation along coastal areas to act as windbreakers and reduce wind speed. - Installation of early warning systems and weather monitoring stations. - Non-Structural Measures: - Community awareness and education programs on cyclone preparedness. - Training of local volunteers and formation of disaster response teams. - Preparation and regular updating of cyclone vulnerability maps. - Formulation of local-level disaster management plans. - Regulation of land use and coastal zone management to avoid unplanned development. - Technological leverage - With the help of development of techniques to monitor the behaviour of cyclones, their intensity, direction and magnitude, it has become possible to manage the cyclonic hazard to some extent - Emergency Response: - Timely evacuation of people from high-risk zones using early warnings. - Pre-positioning of emergency supplies such as food, water, medicines, and rescue equipment. - Setup of temporary shelters and relief camps. - Rapid deployment of search and rescue teams post-cyclone. - Institutional Measures: - Role of the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) and State Disaster Management Authorities. - Coordination between IMD (Indian Meteorological Department) and local administration for timely alerts. - Implementation of the Disaster Management Act, 2005. - Long-Term Strategies: - Coastal zone regulation and climate-resilient infrastructure planning. - Integration of cyclone risk into development planning. - Promoting sustainable livelihoods to reduce long-term vulnerability. - However, increase in the loss of life and property in countries like India, Bangladesh, Myanmar, etc. in successive storms is largely due to high vulnerability of their population residing in the coastal areas - Floods - - - Nature and causes - You read in newspapers and watch images of floods on televisions occurring in some regions during rainy seasons - Inundation of land and human settlements by the rise of water in the channels and its spill-over presents the condition of flooding - Unlike other natural disasters, the causes of floods are well-established - Floods are relatively slow in occurrences and often, occur in well-identified regions and within expected time in a year - Floods occur commonly when water in the form of surface run-off exceeds the carrying capacity of the river channels and streams and flows into the neighbouring low-lying flood plains - At times, this even goes beyond the capacity of lakes and other inland water bodies in which they flow - Floods can also be caused due to a storm surge (in the coastal areas), high intensity rainfall for a considerably longer time period, melting of ice and snow, reduction in the infiltration rate and presence of eroded material in the water due to higher rate of soil erosion - Though floods occur frequently over wide geographical area having disastrous ramifications [a complex or unwelcome consequence of an action or event] in many parts of the world, floods in the South, Southeast and East Asian countries, particularly in China, India and Bangladesh, are frequent and equally disastrous - Human influence - Once again, unlike other natural disasters, human beings play an important role in the genesis as well as spread of floods - Indiscriminate deforestation, unscientific agricultural practices, disturbances along the natural drainage channels and colonisation of flood-plains and river-beds are some of the human activities that play an important role in increasing the intensity, magnitude and gravity of floods - Distribution in India - Various states of India face heavy loss of lives and property due to recurrent floods - Rashtriya Barh Ayog (National Flood Commission) identified 40 million hectares of land as flood-prone in India - The Figure 7.6 shows the flood-affected areas in India - Assam, West Bengal and Bihar are among the high flood-prone states of India - Apart from these, most of the rivers in the northern states like Punjab and Uttar Pradesh, are also vulnerable to occasional floods - It has been noticed that states like Rajasthan, Gujarat, Haryana and Punjab are also getting inundated in recent decades due to flash floods - This is partly because of the pattern of the monsoon and partly because of blocking of most of the streams and river channels by human activities - Sometimes, Tamil Nadu experiences flooding during November-January due to the retreating monsoon - Consequence of floods - Frequent inundation of agricultural land and human settlement, particularly in Assam, West Bengal, Bihar and Eastern Uttar Pradesh (flooding rivers), coastal areas of Odisha, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu and Gujarat (cyclone) and Punjab, Rajasthan, Northern Gujarat and Haryana (flash floods) have serious consequences on the national economy and society - Floods do not only destroy valuable crops every year but these also damage physical infrastructure such as roads, rails, bridges and human settlements - Millions of people are rendered homeless and are also washed down along with their cattle in the floods - Spread of diseases like cholera, gastro-enteritis, hepatitis and other water-borne diseases spread in the flood-affected areas - However, floods also make a few positive contributions - Every year, floods deposit fertile silt over agricultural fields which is good for the crops - Majuli (Assam), the largest riverine island in the world, is the best example of good paddy crops after the annual floods in Brahmaputra - - Other important riverine island examples are: - Umananda Island (Assam) - Bhavani Island (Andhra Pradesh) - - But these are insignificant benefits in comparison to the grave losses - Famous examples -
Flood Year Location Cause Impact
Great China Flood 1931 China (Yangtze, Huai, Yellow Rivers) Heavy rain and river overflow Estimated 1-4 million deaths; massive displacement
Mississippi Flood 1927 USA (Mississippi River Basin) Heavy rainfall and levee failure 500+ deaths; over 600,000 displaced; huge economic loss
Kerala Floods 2018 Kerala, India Unusually heavy monsoon rainfall ~500 deaths; large-scale destruction; millions displaced
Jammu & Kashmir Floods 2014 Jammu & Kashmir, India Heavy rain & cloudburst ~300 deaths; extensive damage to property and infrastructure
Bangladesh Flood 1998 Bangladesh Monsoon and river overflow Over 1,000 deaths; one-third of the country underwater
Venice Flood 1966 Venice, Italy Tidal surge and heavy rains Extensive cultural and structural damage
Uttarakhand Cloudburst & Landslides (Kedarnath Tragedy) 2013 Uttarakhand, India Thousands killed; massive destruction of infrastructure; severe damage to Kedarnath town and surrounding areas due to cloudburst-induced landslides and floods
- Mitigation [the action of reducing the severity, seriousness, or painfulness of something] - The Government of India as well as the state governments are well aware of the menace created by floods every year - Structural Measures: - Construction of dams, reservoirs, and retention basins to regulate river flow. - Embankments and levees to prevent water from overflowing into inhabited areas. - Drainage improvement in urban areas to prevent waterlogging. - Desilting of river beds to increase water-carrying capacity. - Non-Structural Measures: - Floodplain zoning: Restricting construction and settlements in vulnerable areas. - Removal of human encroachment from the river channels and depopulating the flood plains can be the other steps - Development of flood forecasting and early warning systems by IMD and CWC. - Preparation of flood hazard maps and vulnerability assessments. - Insurance mechanisms for flood-prone areas. - Ecosystem-Based Approaches: - Afforestation and watershed management to reduce surface runoff. - Restoration of wetlands and natural floodplains for water absorption. - Rejuvenation of rivers and natural drainage systems. - Community Preparedness: - Public awareness campaigns about do’s and don’ts during floods. - Training of local communities in emergency response and evacuation. - Construction of raised platforms and safe shelters in flood-prone villages. - Institutional Measures: - Role of NDMA and State Disaster Management Authorities in policy, planning, and response. - Flood Management Programme (FMP) under the Ministry of Jal Shakti. - Coordination among IMD, CWC, and state irrigation departments. - Urban Flood Mitigation: - Smart drainage systems and flood sensors in urban planning. - Ban on encroachments over drains and riverbeds. - Promotion of rainwater harvesting and permeable surfaces to reduce runoff. - Cyclone centres may provide relief in coastal areas which are hit by a storm surge - Droughts - - - Nature and causes - The term ‘drought’ is applied to an extended period when there is a shortage of water availability due to inadequate precipitation, excessive rate of evaporation and over-utilisation of water from the reservoirs and other storages, including the ground water - Drought is a complex phenomenon as it involves elements of meteorology like precipitation, evaporation, evapotranspiration, ground water, soil moisture, storage and surface run-off, agricultural practices, particularly the types of crops grown, socio-economic practices and ecological conditions - Types of droughts - Meteorological drought: - It is a situation when there is a prolonged period of inadequate rainfall marked with mal-distribution of the same over time and space - Agricultural drought: - It is also known as soil moisture drought, characterised by low soil moisture that is necessary to support the crops, thereby resulting in crop failures - Moreover, if an area has more than 30 per cent of its gross cropped area under irrigation, the area is excluded from the drought-prone category - Hydrological drought: - It results when the availability of water in different storages and reservoirs like aquifers, lakes, reservoirs, etc. falls below what the precipitation can replenish - Ecological drought: - When the productivity of a natural ecosystem fails due to shortage of water and as a consequence of ecological distress, damages are induced in the ecosystem - Various parts of India experience these droughts recurrently which result in some serious socio-economic and ecological problems - Drought prone areas in India - Indian agriculture has been heavily dependent on the monsoon rainfall - Droughts and floods are the two accompanying features of Indian climate - According to some estimates, nearly 19 per cent of the total geographical area of the country and 12 per cent of its total population suffer due to drought every year - About 30 per cent of the country’s total area is identified as drought prone affecting around 50 million people - It is a common experience that while some parts of the country reel under floods, there are regions that face severe drought during the same period - Moreover, it is also a common sight to witness that one region suffers due to floods in one season and experiences drought in the other - This is mainly because of the large-scale variations and unpredictability in the behaviour of the monsoon in India - Thus, droughts are widespread and common phenomena in most parts of the country, but these are most recurrent and severe in some and not so in others - On the basis of severity of droughts, India can be divided into the following regions: - Extreme drought affected areas: - It is evident from the Figure 7.8 that most parts of Rajasthan, particularly areas to the west of the Aravali hills, i.e. Marusthali and Kachchh regions of Gujarat fall in this category - Included here are also the districts like Jaisalmer and Barmer from the Indian desert that receive less that 90 mm average annual rainfall - Severe drought prone area: - Parts of eastern Rajasthan, most parts of Madhya Pradesh, eastern parts of Maharashtra, interior parts of Andhra Pradesh and Karnataka Plateau, northern parts of interior Tamil Nadu and southern parts of Jharkhand and interior Odisha are included in this category - Moderate drought affected area: - Northern parts of Rajasthan, Haryana, southern districts of Uttar Pradesh, the remaining parts of Gujarat, Maharashtra except Konkan, Jharkhand and Coimbatore plateau of Tamil Nadu and interior Karnataka are included in this category - Less prone - The remaining parts of India can be considered either free or less prone to the drought - Consequences of drought - Droughts have cascading [pass (something) on to a succession of others] effects on various other aspects of environment and society - Crop failure leading to scarcity of food grains (akal), fodder (trinkal), inadequate rainfall, resulting in shortage of water (jalkal), and often shortage in all the three (trikal) is most devastating - Large-scale death of cattle and other animals, migration of humans and livestock are the most common sight to be seen in the drought-affected areas - Scarcity of water compels people to consume contaminated water resulting in spread of many waterborne diseases like gastro-enteritis, cholera, hepatitis, etc. - Droughts have both immediate as well as long-term disastrous consequences on the social and physical environments - Drought vs famine - Drought and famine are distinct but related natural disasters. Drought is a prolonged period of low rainfall leading to water shortages, while famine is a severe shortage of food, often resulting from drought but also from other factors like conflict or economic instability. - While drought can contribute to famine, it's not always the case, as other factors like access to food and resources are also crucial. - Famous examples -
Drought Year(s) Location Cause Impact
Dust Bowl Drought 1930s USA (Great Plains) Severe drought + poor farming practices Massive crop failures, displacement of thousands
Sahel Drought 1968–1985 West Africa (Sahel region) Reduced rainfall and desertification Hundreds of thousands of deaths due to famine
Great Indian Drought 1965–1966 India Monsoon failure Severe food shortages, economic losses, migration
Marathwada Drought 2015 Maharashtra, India Below-normal rainfall Drastic water scarcity, farmer distress, crop loss
Kalahari Drought 2000s Southern Africa Climate variability and reduced precipitation Reduced water availability, impact on livelihoods
- Mitigation - Water Resource Management: - Consequently [as a result], planning for drought has to take both aspects into consideration - Provision for the distribution of safe drinking water, medicines for the victims and availability of fodder and water for the cattle and shifting of the people and their livestock to safer places, etc. are some steps that need to be taken immediately - Rainwater harvesting to recharge groundwater. - Construction of check dams, percolation tanks, and farm ponds. - Watershed management for better moisture conservation. - Efficient irrigation techniques like drip and sprinkler systems. - Agricultural Strategies: - Use of drought-resistant and short-duration crop varieties. - Crop diversification and mixed cropping to reduce risk. - Adjusting cropping patterns based on agro-climatic zones. - Promotion of dryland farming and soil moisture conservation. - Policy & Institutional Measures: - Implementation of Drought-Prone Area Programme (DPAP). - MGNREGA for creating drought-resilient assets like water tanks. - Crop insurance schemes like PMFBY to provide financial support. - Role of NDMA and State Disaster Management Authorities. - Technology and Forecasting: - Identification of ground water potential in the form of aquifers, transfer of river water from the surplus to the deficit areas, and particularly planning for inter-linking of rivers and construction of reservoirs and dams, etc. should be given a serious thought - Remote sensing and satellite imageries can be useful in identifying the possible river-basins that can be inter-linked and in identifying the ground water potential - Early warning systems using satellite data (e.g., ISRO, IMD). - Use of weather forecasts for contingency crop planning. - GIS mapping of vulnerable areas for targeted intervention. - Community Participation: - Dissemination of knowledge about drought-resistant crops and proper training to practise the same can be some of the long-term measures that will be helpful in drought-mitigation - Village-level water budgeting and decentralized planning. - Community-based management of water resources. - Observe the methods adopted for rooftop rainwater harvesting in your locality and suggest measures to make it more effective - Rainwater harvesting can also be an effective method in minimising the effects of drought - Capacity building through awareness and training programmes. - Long-Term Ecological Measures: - Afforestation and protection of catchment areas. - Revival of traditional water bodies and stepwells. - Promotion of millets and less water-intensive crops. - Urban and Drinking Water Security: - Regulation of groundwater extraction through local bodies. - Rooftop rainwater harvesting in urban areas. - Desalination plants in coastal drought-prone cities. - Landslides - - Nature and causes - Have you ever read about the blocking of roads to Srinagar or disruption of rail services by stones falling on the Konkan Railway track? - It happens due to landslide, which is the rapid sliding of large mass of bedrocks - Disasters due to landslides, are in general, far less dramatic than due to earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, tsunamis and cyclones but their impact on the natural environment and national economy is in no way less severe - Unlike other disasters that are sudden, unpredictable and are largely controlled by macro or regional factors, landslides are largely controlled by highly localised factors - Hence, gathering information and monitoring the possibilities of landslide is not only difficult but also immensely cost-intensive - It is always difficult to define in a precise statement and generalise the occurrence and behaviour of a landslide - However, on the basis of past experiences, frequency and certain causal relationships with the controlling factors like geology, geomorphic agents, slope, land-use, vegetation cover and human activities. - India has been divided into a number of zones: - Very high vulnerability zone: - Highly unstable, relatively young mountainous areas in the Himalayas and Andaman and Nicobar, high rainfall regions with steep slopes in the Western Ghats and Nilgiris, the north-eastern regions, along with areas that experience frequent ground-shaking due to earthquakes, etc. and areas of intense human activities, particularly those related to construction of roads, dams, etc. are included in this zone - High vulnerability zone: - Areas that have almost similar conditions to those included in the very high vulnerability zone are also included in this category - The only difference between these two is the combination, intensity and frequency of the controlling factors - All the Himalayan states and the states from the north-eastern regions except the plains of Assam are included in the high vulnerability zones - Moderate to low vulnerability zone: - Areas that receive less precipitation such as Trans-Himalayan areas of Ladakh and Spiti (Himachal Pradesh), undulated yet stable relief and low precipitation areas in the Aravali, rain shadow areas in the Western and Eastern Ghats and Deccan plateau also experience occasional landslides - Landslides due to mining and subsidence are most common in states like Jharkhand, Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Goa and Kerala - Other areas: - The remaining parts of India, particularly states like Rajasthan, Haryana, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, West Bengal (except district Darjiling), Assam (except district Karbi Anglong) and Coastal regions of the southern States are safe as far as landslides are concerned - Consequences of landslides - Landslides have relatively small and localised area of direct influence, but roadblock, destruction of railway lines and channel-blocking due to rock-falls have far-reaching consequences - Diversion of river courses due to landslides can also lead to flood and loss of life and property - It also makes spatial interaction difficult, risky as well as a costly affair, which, in turn, adversely affects the developmental activities in these areas - Famous examples -
Landslide Year Location Cause Impact
Vargas Tragedy 1999 Vargas, Venezuela Heavy rainfall triggering massive landslides Thousands dead, widespread destruction
Oso Landslide 2014 Washington, USA Unstable slope + heavy rainfall 43 deaths, destruction of homes
Manali Landslide 2020 Himachal Pradesh, India Intense rainfall and deforestation Road blockage, property damage
Kangra Valley Landslide 1905 Himachal Pradesh, India Earthquake triggered slope failure Major damage, loss of life
Malin Landslide 2014 Maharashtra, India Deforestation and heavy rainfall More than 150 deaths, village wiped out
- Mitigation - Structural Measures: - Restriction on the construction and other developmental activities such as roads and dams, limiting agriculture to valleys and areas with moderate slopes, and control on the development of large settlements in the high vulnerability zones, should be enforced - Construction of retaining walls, gabion walls, and check dams to stabilize slopes. - Installation of rock bolts and netting to prevent falling debris. - Creation of drainage channels to prevent water accumulation. - Slope terracing and afforestation to reduce surface runoff. - Non-Structural Measures: - Hazard zonation mapping using GIS and remote sensing for identifying vulnerable zones. - Land use regulations to restrict construction and deforestation in high-risk areas. - Early warning systems based on rainfall thresholds and ground sensors. - Community Awareness and Preparedness: - Public education on signs of landslides and emergency response. - Conducting regular mock drills and local-level disaster management training. - Relocation of vulnerable communities from highly unstable slopes. - Environmental Management: - Avoiding large-scale excavation, quarrying, and construction on fragile slopes. - Promoting eco-friendly hill development and bioengineering solutions. - Reforestation and soil-binding vegetation to enhance slope stability. - Terrace farming should be encouraged in the northeastern hill states where Jhumming (Slash and Burn/Shifting Cultivation) is still prevalent - Technological and Institutional Measures: - Use of LiDAR, InSAR, and UAVs (drones) for real-time monitoring. - Establishment of National Centre for Landslide Research and Mitigation under NDMA. - Integration of landslide data with disaster risk reduction plans. - Region-Specific Initiatives: - In Himalayas and Western Ghats: Micro-zonation and check on hydro projects. - In North-East: Stabilization of jhum cultivation areas and road alignment planning. - Disaster management - Disaster management refers to organized efforts to prepare for, respond to, mitigate, and recover from disasters — both natural and man-made. - Unlike sudden and unpredictable events like earthquakes or tsunamis, some disasters such as tropical cyclones have relatively better predictability regarding their time and location. - Advances in meteorological and remote sensing technologies allow early warning systems to monitor cyclone intensity, direction, and trajectory, enabling better preparedness. - Key Disaster Management Measures - Structural Measures: - Construction of cyclone shelters to protect vulnerable coastal populations. - Building embankments, dykes, and reservoirs to control floods and storm surges. - Afforestation and mangrove restoration to reduce wind speed and coastal erosion. - Implementation of earthquake-resistant construction codes to reduce structural damage. - Non-Structural Measures: - Early warning dissemination and public awareness campaigns. - Evacuation planning and drills for high-risk communities. - Land-use planning to avoid settlements in vulnerable zones like flood plains and landslide-prone areas. - Training and capacity building for disaster response teams. - Vulnerability and Challenges - Countries like India, Bangladesh, and Myanmar experience repeated cyclones with increasing loss of life and property, mainly due to high population density in low-lying coastal areas. - Rapid urbanization, unplanned settlements, and inadequate infrastructure exacerbate vulnerability. - Climate change is intensifying the frequency and severity of many hazards, including floods, droughts, and cyclones, complicating disaster management efforts. - Legal Framework: Disaster Management Act, 2005 - Defines disaster as a catastrophe or calamity arising from natural or man-made causes that results in substantial loss of life, human suffering, or environmental damage, and is beyond the coping capacity of the affected community. - Establishes the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) as the apex body for disaster management in India, chaired by the Prime Minister. - Mandates the creation of State and District Disaster Management Authorities to implement policies at local levels. - Provides legal provisions for disaster risk reduction, preparedness, response, and rehabilitation. - Stages of Disaster Management - 1. Pre-Disaster (Preparedness and Mitigation): - Hazard assessment and risk mapping to identify vulnerable areas. - Creation of early warning systems and communication networks. - Community awareness programs and capacity building. - Formulation of disaster management plans and resource mobilization. 2. During Disaster (Response): - Rapid mobilization of rescue and relief operations. - Evacuation of affected populations to safe zones or shelters. - Provision of emergency supplies: food, water, medicines, and temporary shelter. - Coordination among various government and non-government agencies for effective response. 3. Post-Disaster (Recovery and Rehabilitation): - Restoration of basic services such as water supply, electricity, and sanitation. - Psychological support and rehabilitation of affected individuals and communities. - Reconstruction of infrastructure with improved disaster-resilient standards. - Review and updating of disaster management plans based on lessons learned. - Importance for India - About two-thirds of India’s geographical area and population are vulnerable to multiple natural hazards, including floods, droughts, cyclones, earthquakes, and landslides. - The diverse climate and topography make disaster management complex and require region-specific strategies. - Institutional strengthening through NDMA, State Disaster Management Authorities, and National Institute of Disaster Management (NIDM) has improved coordination and capacity. - Integration of traditional knowledge with modern scientific methods enhances community resilience. - Conclusion - Disasters are inevitable, but their impacts can be minimized through effective preparedness, mitigation, and timely response. - Holistic disaster management combines structural and non-structural approaches, emphasizing community participation and sustainable development. - Continuous improvement in technology, infrastructure, governance, and awareness is key to reducing disaster risks in India and globally.
- India physical PYQs - [1995] - Q1: Alpine vegetation in the Western Himalayas is found only up to a height of 3000 metres, while in the Eastern Himalayas, it is found up to 4000 metres. The reason for this variation in the same mountain range is: - Options: (a) Eastern Himalayas are higher than Western Himalayas (b) Eastern Himalayas are nearer to the equator and sea coast than Western Himalayas (c) Eastern Himalayas receive more monsoon rainfall than Western Himalayas (d) Eastern Himalayan rocks are more fertile than Western Himalayan rocks - Answer: - (c) Eastern Himalayas receive more monsoon rainfall than Western Himalayas - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - Alpine vegetation, consisting of grasslands and shrubs above the tree line, grows in high-altitude, cold environments. In the Himalayas, the altitude of the alpine zone varies due to climatic factors. - The Eastern Himalayas (e.g., Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh) receive heavy monsoon rainfall (200-400 cm annually) from the Bay of Bengal branch, allowing vegetation to thrive up to 4000 m. - The Western Himalayas (e.g., Jammu & Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh) receive less rainfall (50-100 cm), mainly from western disturbances, restricting alpine vegetation to 3000 m. - Explanation: - Option (c) is correct because higher monsoon rainfall in the Eastern Himalayas supports a higher tree line and alpine zone by maintaining soil moisture and moderating temperatures. This allows vegetation to persist at higher altitudes compared to the drier Western Himalayas. - Why Not Others?: - (a) Eastern Himalayas are higher than Western Himalayas: - Incorrect. Both regions have comparable peak heights (e.g., Kanchenjunga, 8586 m, in the East; Nanga Parbat, 8126 m, in the West). Altitude differences do not explain the vegetation variation; rainfall does. - (b) Eastern Himalayas are nearer to the equator and sea coast than Western Himalayas: - Incorrect. While Eastern Himalayas are slightly closer to the equator (e.g., Arunachal at ~27°N vs. Kashmir at ~34°N) and the Bay of Bengal, this primarily increases rainfall, which is the direct cause (c). Proximity alone is insufficient. - (d) Eastern Himalayan rocks are more fertile than Western Himalayan rocks: - Incorrect. Alpine vegetation depends on climate (moisture, temperature), not rock fertility. Soils in alpine zones are generally thin and rocky across the Himalayas. - Related Concepts: - Monsoon Patterns: The Bay of Bengal monsoon branch delivers heavy rainfall to the Eastern Himalayas, while western disturbances bring limited precipitation to the West. - Vegetation Zones: Himalayan zones include tropical, temperate, and alpine, with rainfall influencing their altitudinal limits. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on Himalayan ecology (e.g., Q7 [1996] on equatorial rainfall) or biodiversity (e.g., Q2 [1995] on biosphere reserves). Tests understanding of climatic influences on vegetation, a key UPSC topic. - Key Takeaway: - Higher monsoon rainfall in the Eastern Himalayas enables alpine vegetation to extend to 4000 m, unlike the drier Western Himalayas, where it is limited to 3000 m. - Q2: Consider the map given below: - - The division along India's coastal region in the given map indicates: - Options: (a) Coastal pollution zones (b) Salinity density isopleths (c) Areas up to which Indian sovereignty extends (d) Underwater relief contours - Answer: - (d) Underwater relief contours - Q3: In the given map, three out of four marked places indicate locations of thermal power stations. The place without a thermal power station is marked: - - Options: (a) 1 (b) 2 (c) 3 (d) 4 - Answer: - (d) 4 - Q4: "Monoculture of commercially viable trees is destroying the unique natural profile. Thoughtless exploitation of timber, deforesting vast tracts for palm cultivation, destruction of mangroves, illegal logging by tribals, and poaching only compound the problem. Fresh water pockets are fast drying up due to deforestation and destruction of mangroves." The place referred to in this quotation is: - Options: (a) Sundarban (b) Kerala coast (c) Orissa coast (d) Andaman and Nicobar Islands - Answer: - (a) Sundarban - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - The Sundarbans, a UNESCO World Heritage Site in West Bengal and Bangladesh, is a deltaic mangrove ecosystem formed by the Ganga, Brahmaputra, and Meghna rivers, hosting unique biodiversity (e.g., Royal Bengal Tiger). In the 1990s, it faced monoculture plantations (e.g., eucalyptus for pulp), illegal timber extraction, mangrove clearing for aquaculture and agriculture (e.g., shrimp farming, coconut plantations), logging by local communities (sometimes termed "tribal" in older contexts), and poaching (tigers, fish). These disrupted freshwater inflows, as mangroves regulate salinity. - Explanation: - Option (a) is correct: The Sundarbans align with the quotation’s 1990s issues: - Monoculture: Eucalyptus plantations reduced mangrove diversity. - Timber exploitation: Illegal mangrove logging occurred despite conservation. - Palm cultivation: Coconut and betel plantations, plus aquaculture, cleared mangroves, fitting the broad "palm cultivation" term in 1995 context. - Mangrove destruction: Shrimp farming and settlements reduced mangrove cover. - Illegal logging by tribals: Local communities logged for fuelwood. - Poaching: Tigers and fish were poached. - Freshwater depletion: Mangrove loss and upstream damming (e.g., Farakka Barrage) increased salinity, drying freshwater pockets. - 1990s reports (e.g., WWF, Sundarban Biosphere Reserve) confirm these issues. - Why Not Others?: - (b) Kerala coast: - Incorrect. Coconut plantations dominate; commercial tree monoculture (e.g., eucalyptus) and palm oil are minimal. Mangrove loss is less tied to freshwater depletion. - (c) Orissa coast: - Incorrect. Odisha’s coast (Bhitarkanika) has mangroves, but monoculture and palm cultivation are negligible. Freshwater issues relate to erosion. - (d) Andaman and Nicobar Islands: - Incorrect: The analysis’ choice. The Andamans faced logging (teak) and mangrove loss, but monoculture was limited, and palm cultivation was experimental. Tribal logging (e.g., Onge) was minor, and freshwater issues were island-specific, not deltaic. - Related Concepts: - Mangrove Ecosystems: Regulate salinity and freshwater in deltas. - Deltaic Challenges: Aquaculture and damming exacerbate degradation. - Broader Connection: - Links to Q2 [1995] on biosphere reserves, Q8 [1996] on mangroves, Q219 [2017] on wetlands. - Key Takeaway: - The Sundarbans’ 1990s environmental crisis (monoculture, mangrove loss, freshwater depletion) matches the quotation, unlike the Andamans. - Q5: Palaeomagnetic results obtained from India indicate that, in the past, the Indian landmass moved: - Options: (a) Northward (b) Southward (c) Eastward (d) Westward - Answer: - (a) Northward - Analysis: - Scientific Context: - Palaeomagnetism studies the Earth’s magnetic field recorded in rocks, revealing past positions of tectonic plates. India’s palaeomagnetic data show that the Indian Plate, once part of Gondwana near the South Pole, moved northward over millions of years, colliding with the Eurasian Plate ~50 million years ago to form the Himalayas. - Explanation: - Option (a) is correct because palaeomagnetic evidence from Indian rocks (e.g., Deccan Traps) indicates a northward drift from the Southern Hemisphere (near Antarctica) to its current position (20–30°N). This movement, at ~15 cm/year historically, caused the Himalayan orogeny. - Why Not Others?: - (b) Southward: - Incorrect. India’s geological history shows northward movement from Gondwana, not southward. - (c) Eastward: - Incorrect. While India experienced minor lateral shifts, the dominant motion was northward, not eastward. - (d) Westward: - Incorrect. No significant westward movement is supported; the Indian Plate’s trajectory was primarily northward. - Related Concepts: - Plate Tectonics: The Indian Plate’s northward drift is a classic example, driven by seafloor spreading in the Indian Ocean. - Himalayan Formation: Resulted from India-Eurasia collision, creating the world’s highest range. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on geological processes (e.g., Q15 [1997] on Mars’ conditions) or Himalayan geography (e.g., Q1 [1995] on vegetation). Tests understanding of tectonics, a key UPSC topic. - Key Takeaway: - Palaeomagnetic data confirm India’s northward movement from Gondwana, shaping its current geography and the Himalayas. - Q6: Of the four places marked 1, 2, 3, and 4 on the given map, the one indicating a tidal port is: - - Options: (a) 1 (b) 2 (c) 3 (d) 4 - Answer: - (d) - Q7: The Indian subcontinent was originally a part of a huge mass called: - Options: (a) Jurassic land mass (b) Aryavarta (c) Indiana (d) Gondwana continent - Answer: - (d) Gondwana continent - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - The Indian subcontinent was part of Gondwana, a supercontinent in the Southern Hemisphere that existed ~800–180 million years ago. Gondwana included present-day India, Africa, Antarctica, Australia, and South America, centered around the South Pole. The Indian Plate broke off from Gondwana ~140 million years ago, drifting northward to collide with Eurasia, forming the Himalayas. - Explanation: - Option (d) is correct because geological and palaeomagnetic evidence confirms India’s origin in Gondwana. Fossils (e.g., Glossopteris flora) and rock formations (e.g., Gondwana Supergroup) in India match those in other Gondwana continents, supporting this history. - Why Not Others?: - (a) Jurassic land mass: - Incorrect. The Jurassic period (201–145 million years ago) was a geological time frame, not a specific landmass. Gondwana existed during and beyond the Jurassic. - (b) Aryavarta: - Incorrect. Aryavarta is a cultural/historical term from ancient Indian texts, referring to northern India, not a geological landmass. - (c) Indiana: - Incorrect. “Indiana” is not a recognized geological term; it’s likely a distractor or refers to the U.S. state, unrelated to India’s geological history. - Related Concepts: - Plate Tectonics: India’s northward drift from Gondwana is a key example, driven by seafloor spreading. - Gondwana Fossils: Shared flora and fauna (e.g., Lystrosaurus) link India to other Gondwana continents. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on tectonics (e.g., Q5 [1995] on India’s northward movement) or Himalayan formation (e.g., Q1 [1995] on Himalayan vegetation). Tests geological history, a core UPSC topic. - Key Takeaway: - The Indian subcontinent’s origin in the Gondwana continent explains its geological and fossil record, distinguishing it from cultural or irrelevant terms. - Q8: Which one of the following mountain ranges is spread over only one state in India? [1995] - Options: (a) Aravalli (b) Satpura (c) Ajanta (d) Sahyadri - Answer: - (c) Ajanta - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - India’s mountain ranges often span multiple states, but the Ajanta Range, a low hill range in Maharashtra, is confined to one state. It’s known for the Ajanta Caves, a UNESCO World Heritage Site with ancient Buddhist rock-cut temples. - Explanation: - Option (c) is correct because the Ajanta Range is located entirely in Maharashtra, specifically in the Aurangabad district. Other ranges listed span multiple states, making Ajanta unique in this context. - Why Not Others?: - (a) Aravalli: - Incorrect. The Aravalli Range extends across Rajasthan, Haryana, Gujarat, and Delhi, covering multiple states. - (b) Satpura: - Incorrect. The Satpura Range spans Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, and Chhattisgarh (partly Gujarat in some definitions), not one state. - (d) Sahyadri: - Incorrect. Sahyadri, another name for the Western Ghats, runs through Maharashtra, Goa, Karnataka, Kerala, and Tamil Nadu, across multiple states. - Related Concepts: - Indian Physiography: India’s ranges vary in geological origin (e.g., Aravalli is ancient, Western Ghats are escarpments). - Ajanta Caves: Cultural significance enhances the range’s prominence in UPSC questions. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on Indian geography (e.g., Q1 [1995] on Himalayas) or cultural sites (e.g., Q2 [1995] on biosphere reserves). This pressure gradient draws moist winds from the southwest, known as the south-west monsoon, bringing 70–90% of India’s annual rainfall. - Explanation: - Option (b) is correct because the south-west monsoon is driven by the summer low-pressure system over India, carrying moisture from the Indian Ocean, leading to heavy rainfall across the subcontinent. - Why Not Others?: - (a) South-east monsoon: - Incorrect. No south-east monsoon exists in India; southeast winds are associated with other regions (e.g., Southeast Asia). - (c) Trade winds: - Incorrect. Trade winds are steady easterly winds in the tropics, not seasonal or tied to India’s summer monsoon. - (d) Westerlies: - Incorrect. Westerlies are mid-latitude winds from the west, affecting India in winter via western disturbances, not summer. - Related Concepts: - Monsoon Mechanism: Driven by differential heating and the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) shift. - Rainfall Patterns: South-west monsoon affects agriculture, water resources, and economy. - Broader Connection: - Links to Q7 [1996] on equatorial rainfall or Q19 [1996] on climatic conditions. Tests monsoon dynamics, a core UPSC topic. - Key Takeaway: - The south-west monsoon, triggered by summer low pressure, is critical for India’s rainfall, distinguishing it from other wind systems. - Q16: Which one of the following rivers thrice forks into two streams and reunites a few miles farther on, thus forming the islands of Srirangapattanam, Sivasamudram, and Srirangam? - Options: (a) Cauvery (b) Tungabhadra (c) Krishna (d) Godavari - Answer: - (a) Cauvery - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - The Cauvery River, originating in the Western Ghats (Karnataka), flows through Tamil Nadu, forming islands by splitting and rejoining due to its braided channel. Srirangapattanam, Sivasamudram, and Srirangam are culturally and geographically significant islands formed by this process. - Explanation: - Option (a) is correct. The Cauvery forks thrice: at Srirangapattanam (Karnataka), Sivasamudram (Karnataka, site of a waterfall and hydroelectric plant), and Srirangam (Tamil Nadu, famous for its temple), creating these islands through its branching and reuniting. - Why Not Others?: - (b) Tungabhadra: - Incorrect. Tungabhadra (Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh) doesn’t form these specific islands. - (c) Krishna: - Incorrect. Krishna forms islands but not Srirangapattanam, Sivasamudram, or Srirangam. - (d) Godavari: - Incorrect. North-east monsoon - 2. Altitude - 3. Western depressions - 4. Distance from the sea - 5. Latitude - Options: (a) A-1, B-2, C-4, D-3 (b) A-4, B-5, C-1, D-3 (c) A-5, B-2, C-4, D-3 (d) A-5, B-1, C-3, D-4 - Answer: - (c) A-5, B-2, C-4, D-3 - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - India’s climate varies due to latitude, altitude, distance from the sea, and seasonal winds, influencing temperature, snowfall, and rainfall patterns. - Explanation: - Option (c) is correct: - A. Tests climatic geography. - Key Takeaway: - Matching climatic conditions to reasons (latitude, altitude, distance, western depressions) explainsಮ - [1996] - Q20: Match List-I (Geological periods) with List-II (Formations) and select the correct answer: - List-I: - A. Deccan Traps - B. Western Ghats - C. Aravalli - D. Narmada-Tapi alluvial deposits - List-II: - 1. Late Cenozoic - 2. Pre-Cambrian - 3. Cretaceous Eocene - 4. Cambrian - 5. Pleistocene - Options: (a) A-3, B-5, C-1, D-4 (b) A-3, B-1, C-2, D-5 (c) A-2, B-1, C-3, D-4 (d) A-1, B-4, C-2, D-5 - Answer: - (b) A-3, B-1, C-2, D-5 - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - India’s geological formations span various periods, reflecting its tectonic history. The Deccan Traps, Western Ghats, Aravalli, and Narmada-Tapi deposits formed in distinct geological epochs, critical for understanding India’s physiography. - Explanation: - Option (b) is correct: - A. Deccan Traps: Cretaceous-Eocene (3, ~66–55 Ma), massive volcanic basalt flows in western India. - B. Western Ghats: Late Cenozoic (1, ~23–2.6 Ma), uplifted escarpments due to tectonic activity. - C. Aravalli: Pre-Cambrian (2, >541 Ma), ancient folded mountains in Rajasthan. - D. Narmada-Tapi alluvial deposits: Pleistocene (5, ~2.6 Ma–11,700 years), recent sedimentary deposits in river valleys. - Why Not Others?: - (a) A-3, B-5, C-1, D-4: - Incorrect. Deccan Traps are not Pre-Cambrian (A-2); Aravalli is not Cretaceous-Eocene (C-3); Narmada-Tapi is not Cambrian (D-4). - (d) A-1, B-4, C-2, D-5: - Incorrect. In India, the first biosphere reserves were established in the 1980s. - By 1995, India had several biosphere reserves, including Great Nicobar (1989), Sunderbans (1989), and Nanda Devi (1988), recognized for their unique ecosystems and biodiversity. - Why Not Others?: - (a) Great Nicobar: - Correctly included—Great Nicobar Biosphere Reserve, in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, protects tropical rainforests, coral reefs, and endemic species like the Nicobar megapode. - (b) Sunderbans: - Correctly included—Sunderbans Biosphere Reserve, in West Bengal, is famous for its mangrove forests and the Royal Bengal Tiger, designated for its unique estuarine ecosystem. - (c) Nanda Devi: - Correctly included—Nanda Devi Biosphere Reserve, in Uttarakhand, encompasses the Nanda Devi National Park, protecting Himalayan biodiversity, including snow leopards and musk deer. - (d) Gulf of Kutch: - Incorrect—While the Gulf of Kutch in Gujarat is ecologically significant (later designated as a Marine National Park), it was not a UNESCO biosphere reserve by 1995. It is known for coral reefs and marine biodiversity but lacked biosphere status at the time. - Related Concepts: - Biosphere Reserves in India: - Other early reserves include Nilgiri (1986), Nokrek (1988), and Manas (1989), reflecting India’s commitment to biodiversity conservation. - Gulf of Kutch: - Later recognized for its marine biodiversity, it was proposed as a biosphere reserve post-1995, but not included in the initial MAB list. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on conservation (e.g., Q31 [2001] on Gulf of Mannar as a biosphere reserve) or India’s ecological hotspots (e.g., Q7 [1996] on Western Ghats). - Reflects India’s role in global biodiversity frameworks like UNESCO’s MAB Programme. - Key Takeaway: - Gulf of Kutch, despite its ecological importance, was not among India’s biosphere reserves in 1995, unlike Great Nicobar, Sunderbans, and Nanda Devi, highlighting the specificity of UNESCO designations. - [1996] - Q6: Consider the map given below: - - The dotted (broken) line in the map is the: - Options: (a) Durand line (b) McMohan line (c) Line of demarcation between India and Pakistan suggested by the Boundary Commission (1947) (d) Route followed by Young Husband’s expedition - Answer: (a) Durand line - Q7: Which one of the following regions of India is now regarded as an ‘ecological hot spot’? - Options: (a) 2°30' W (b) 82°30' E (c) 92°30' W (d) 92°30' E - Answer: (d) 92°30' E - Analysis: - Geographical Context: - The first sunrise of 2000 (January 1) occurred at India’s easternmost point, Andaman and Nicobar Islands, near 92°30' E, ahead of the standard meridian (82°30' E, IST). - Sunlight moves west as Earth rotates east, making 92°30' E the first in India. - Why Not Others?: - (a) 2°30' W: - Incorrect—Western longitude, irrelevant for India. - (b) 82°30' E: - Incorrect—India’s standard meridian, not easternmost. - (c) 92°30' W: - Incorrect—Western hemisphere, far from India. - Related Concepts: - Indian Standard Time: - 82°30' E, but Andaman is ahead at ~92°30' E. - Longitudinal Time: - 15° longitude ≈ 1 hour time difference. - Broader Connection: - Links to time zones (e.g., Q13 [1997] on IST and IDL) or geographical extremities, key for UPSC geography. - Relevant to longitudinal time calculations. - Key Takeaway: - 92°30' E in Andaman and Nicobar saw India’s first sunrise of 2000, due to its eastern position. - [2001] - Q28: The above map is the Union Territory of: - - Options: (a) Chandigarh (b) Daman and Diu (c) Dadra and Nagar Haveli (d) Pondicherry - Answer: (c) Dadra and Nagar Haveli - Q31: Consider the following statements regarding environment issues in India: - Statements: 1. Environmental Information System (ENVIS) acts as a decentralized information network for environment information - Which of these statements are correct? - Options: (a) 1, 2, and 4 (b) 1, 2, 3, and 4 (c) 2 and 3 (d) 1, 3, and 4 - Answer: (b) 1, 2, 3, and 4 - Analysis: - Environmental Context: - 1.
